 A Lam–Postnikov–Pylyavskyy inequality for hybrid Grothendieck polynomials 


 
 
 
 Peter L. Guo, Mingyang Kang, Jiaji Liu



 


 Abstract

 
 We prove a multivariate Lam–Postnikov–Pylyavskyy type inequality for hybrid Grothendieck polynomials, unifying and refining results for stable and dual stable Grothendieck polynomials established by Chan–Chen–Pak–Soskin.
We also conjecture extensions of the Lam–Postnikov–Pylyavskyy inequality and a conjecture by Thomas–Yong to the (equivariant) Schubert and Grothendieck polynomial setting. 

 


 
 1 Introduction


 
 1.1 Lam–Postnikov–Pylyavskyy inequality


 
 The Lam–Postnikov–Pylyavskyy (LPP) inequality [ 16 ] is a celebrated result in the Schur positivity and log-concavity phenomenon. For partitions λ = ( λ 1 ≥ λ 2 ≥ ⋯ ) \lambda=(\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots) and μ = ( μ 1 ≥ μ 2 ≥ ⋯ ) \mu=(\mu_{1}\geq\mu_{2}\geq\cdots) , define their join λ ∨ μ \lambda\vee\mu and meet λ ∧ μ \lambda\wedge\mu as

 

 
 
 ( λ ∨ μ ) i = max ⁡ { λ i , μ i } , ( λ ∧ μ ) i = min ⁡ { λ i , μ i } . (\lambda\vee\mu)_{i}=\max\{\lambda_{i},\mu_{i}\},\hskip 20.00003pt(\lambda\wedge\mu)_{i}=\min\{\lambda_{i},\mu_{i}\}. 
 
 
 
 Let s λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}) be the Schur function associated with λ \lambda . For two symmetric functions f f and g g , write f ≤ s g f\leq_{s}g if g − f g-f is Schur positive, namely,

 

 
 
 g − f ∈ ∑ λ ℝ ≥ 0 ⋅ s λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) . g-f\in\sum_{\lambda}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\cdot s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}). 
 
 
 
 The Schur LPP inequality states that



 
 Theorem 1.1 ( [ 16 , Theorem 5] ) .

 
 For partitions λ \lambda and μ \mu , we have 

 

 
 
 s λ ( 𝐱 ) s μ ( 𝐱 ) ≤ s s λ ∨ μ ( 𝐱 ) s λ ∧ μ ( 𝐱 ) . s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})s_{\mu}(\mathbf{x})\leq_{s}s_{\lambda\vee\mu}(\mathbf{x})s_{\lambda\wedge\mu}(\mathbf{x}). 
 
 
 




 
 For more thorough information about LPP type inequalities as well as general Schur positivity and log-concavity, we refer to the recent breakthrough work by Speyer [ 24 ] and Chan–Chen–Pak–Soskin [ 4 ] .



 
 As an advanced extension of the LPP inequality, Chan, Chen, Pak and Soskin [ 4 , Theorem 1.6] developed the Schur positivity version of the Ahlswede–Daykin inequality. As the main application, they resolved a Schur positivity conjecture concerning stable Grothendieck polynomials made by Mihalcea. Stable Grothendieck polynomials G λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) G_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}) are the central combinatorial object in the study of Schubert calculus in the K-theory of Grassmannians. A notable combinatorial construction of G λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) G_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}) was given by Buch [ 3 ] by using set-valued tableaux.



 
 Let G ~ λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) = ( − 1 ) | λ | ​ G λ ​ ( − 𝐱 ) \tilde{G}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})=(-1)^{|\lambda|}G_{\lambda}(-\mathbf{x}) be the modification such that each monomial has nonnegative coefficient. The following was conjectured by Mihalcea [ 21 ] .



 
 Theorem 1.2 ( [ 4 , Theorem 1.8] ) .

 
 For partitions λ \lambda and μ \mu , we have 

 

 
 
 G ~ λ ( 𝐱 ) G ~ μ ( 𝐱 ) ≤ s G ~ λ ∨ μ ( 𝐱 ) G ~ λ ∧ μ ( 𝐱 ) . \widetilde{G}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})\widetilde{G}_{\mu}(\mathbf{x})\leq_{s}\widetilde{G}_{\lambda\vee\mu}(\mathbf{x})\widetilde{G}_{\lambda\wedge\mu}(\mathbf{x}). 
 
 
 




 
 Recall that the Hall inner product on symmetric functions is defined by letting < s λ , s μ > = δ λ , μ <s_{\lambda},s_{\mu}>=\delta_{\lambda,\mu} . The orthogonal dual to G λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) G_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}) , denoted g λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) g_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}) , was systematically investigated by Lam and Pylyavskyy [ 17 ] which can be combinatorially generated by reverse plane partitions. The following dual stable Grothendieck LPP inequality is another application of the Ahlswede–Daykin–Schur inequality.



 
 Theorem 1.3 ( [ 4 , Theorem 1.10] ) .

 
 For partitions λ \lambda and μ \mu , we have 

 

 
 
 g λ ( 𝐱 ) g μ ( 𝐱 ) ≤ s g λ ∨ μ ( 𝐱 ) g λ ∧ μ ( 𝐱 ) . g_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})g_{\mu}(\mathbf{x})\leq_{s}g_{\lambda\vee\mu}(\mathbf{x})g_{\lambda\wedge\mu}(\mathbf{x}). 
 
 
 






 
 1.2 Main result


 
 The hybrid Grothendieck polynomial H λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) H_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}) with two families of parameters 𝐭 \mathbf{t} and 𝐰 \mathbf{w} is the generating function

 

 
 
 H λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) = ∑ T ∈ SVRPP ⁡ ( λ ) 𝐱 ircont ⁡ ( T ) ​ 𝐭 ceq ⁡ ( T ) ​ 𝐰 ex ⁡ ( T ) H_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w})=\sum_{T\in\SVRPP(\lambda)}\mathbf{x}^{\mathrm{ircont}(T)}\mathbf{t}^{\ceq(T)}\mathbf{w}^{\ex(T)} 
 
 
 
 over set-valued reverse plane partitions of shape λ \lambda . This class of symmetric functions was introduced by the authors [ 13 ] originally in order to unify stable and dual stable Grothendieck polynomials. More specifically,



 
 Remark 1.4.

 
 We have the following specializations. 

 
 • 
 
 t i = 0 t_{i}=0 for i ≥ 1 i\geq 1 : H λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝟎 , 𝐰 ) {H}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{0},\mathbf{w}) is the refined stable Grothendieck polynomial G λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐰 ) G_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{w}) defined by Chan and Pflueger [ 6 ] . If further setting every w i = − 1 w_{i}=-1 , then H λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝟎 , − 𝟏 ) {H}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{0},\mathbf{-1}) is equal to G λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) G_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}) . Note also that H λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝟎 , 𝟏 ) = G ~ λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) {H}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1})=\widetilde{G}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}) . 




 • 
 
 w i = 0 w_{i}=0 for i ≥ 1 i\geq 1 : H λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐭 , 𝟎 ) {H}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t},\mathbf{0}) is the refined dual stable Grothendieck polynomial g λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐭 ) g_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t}) defined by Galashin, Grinberg and Liu [ 11 ] . If further setting every t i = 1 t_{i}=1 , then H λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝟏 , 𝟎 ) {H}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{1},\mathbf{0}) is equal to g λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) g_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}) . 




 




 
 Though known properties suggest that H λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) H_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}) possesses remarkable properties, resembling those owned by stable and dual stable Grothendieck polynomial, some problems and conjectures are still open in the hybrid setting [ 13 ] .



 
 We extend the ordinary Schur positivity to a polynomial level. Suppose that f f and g g are symmetric functions in 𝐱 \mathbf{x} , with coefficients being polynomials in 𝐳 = ( z 1 , z 2 , … ) \mathbf{z}=(z_{1},z_{2},\ldots) .
Write f ≤ s 𝐳 g f\leq_{s}^{\mathbf{z}}g 
if

 

 
 
 g − f ∈ ∑ λ ℝ ≥ 0 ​ [ 𝐳 ] ⋅ s λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) . g-f\in\sum_{\lambda}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}[\mathbf{z}]\cdot s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}). 
 
 
 


 
 Our main result is stated as follows.



 
 Theorem 1.5.

 
 For partitions λ \lambda and μ \mu , we have 

 

 
 
 H λ ( 𝐱 ; 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) H μ ( 𝐱 ; 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) ≤ s 𝐭 , 𝐰 H λ ∨ μ ( 𝐱 ; 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) H λ ∧ μ ( 𝐱 ; 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) . H_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w})H_{\mu}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w})\leq_{s}^{\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}}H_{\lambda\vee\mu}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w})H_{\lambda\wedge\mu}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}). 
 
 (1.1) 
 
 




 
 By Remark 1.4 , we see that Theorem 1.5 unifies Theorems 1.2 and 1.3 for stable and dual stable Grothendieck polynomials.



 
 Taking 𝐭 = 𝟎 \mathbf{t}=\mathbf{0} in H λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) H_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}) gives the refined stable Grothendieck polynomial G λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐰 ) G_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{w}) of Chan and Pflueger [ 6 ] . In this case, Theorem 1.5 leads to a refinement of Theorem 1.2 .



 
 Corollary 1.6 (refined version of Theorem 1.2 ) .

 
 For partitions λ \lambda and μ \mu , we have 

 

 
 
 G λ ( 𝐱 ; 𝐰 ) G μ ( 𝐱 ; 𝐰 ) ≤ s 𝐰 G λ ∨ μ ( 𝐱 ; 𝐰 ) G λ ∧ μ ( 𝐱 ; 𝐰 ) . G_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{w})G_{\mu}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{w})\leq_{s}^{\mathbf{w}}G_{\lambda\vee\mu}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{w})G_{\lambda\wedge\mu}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{w}). 
 
 
 




 
 Accordingly, taking 𝐰 = 𝟎 \mathbf{w}=\mathbf{0} in Theorem 1.5 yields a LPP inequality for the refined dual stable Grothendieck polynomial g λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐭 ) g_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t}) of Galashin, Grinberg and Liu [ 11 ] .



 
 Corollary 1.7 (refined version of Theorem 1.3 ) .

 
 For partitions λ \lambda and μ \mu , we have 

 

 
 
 g λ ( 𝐱 ; 𝐭 ) g μ ( 𝐱 ; 𝐭 ) ≤ s 𝐭 g λ ∨ μ ( 𝐱 ; 𝐭 ) g λ ∧ μ ( 𝐱 ; 𝐭 ) . g_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t})g_{\mu}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t})\leq_{s}^{\mathbf{t}}g_{\lambda\vee\mu}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t})g_{\lambda\wedge\mu}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t}). 
 
 
 




 
 Note that both Corollaries 1.6 and 1.7 specialize to Theorem 1.1 by taking 𝐰 = 𝟎 \mathbf{w}=\mathbf{0} and 𝐭 = 𝟎 \mathbf{t}=\mathbf{0} , respectively.



 
 Our proof of Theorem 1.5 seems highly nontrivial. The arguments for Theorems 1.2 and 1.3 in [ 4 ] do not seem to directly apply to Theorem 1.5 . Specifically, we will encounter two difficulties which will be briefly explained as follows. Write

 

 
 
 H λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) = ∑ μ K λ , μ ​ ( 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) ​ s μ ​ ( 𝐱 ) . H_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w})=\sum_{\mu}K_{\lambda,\mu}(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w})s_{\mu}(\mathbf{x}). 
 
 
 
 
 • 
 
 The proofs of Theorems 1.2 and 1.3 technically rely on tableau formulas for the coefficients in the Schur expansion of stable or dual stable Grothendieck polynomials [ 4 ] . There exists a combinatorial formula for the coefficients K λ , μ ​ ( 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) K_{\lambda,\mu}(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}) using the language of lattice words [ 13 , Theorem 1.3] . Unfortunately, this formula for K λ , μ ​ ( 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) K_{\lambda,\mu}(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}) does not allow us to use similar arguments as in the proofs of Theorems 1.2 and 1.3 . The essential obstruction is that the lattice word model lacks the structure of a distributive lattice.




 • 
 
 Another key tool used to prove Theorems 1.2 and 1.3 is the Ahlswede–Daykin–Schur inequality developed in [ 4 , Theorem 1.6] . Intuitively, to acquire a proof of Theorem 1.5 , we need a ‘multivariate’ version of the Ahlswede–Daykin–Schur inequality. However, we do not find an appropriate multivariate extension that is applicable to Theorem 1.5 .




 
 In order to address the above issues, we require substantial machinery.



 
 
 • 
 
 We establish a new formula for the coefficients K λ , μ ​ ( 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) K_{\lambda,\mu}(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}) in terms of oscillating sequences, see Corollary 3.5 . The idea behind this is to incorporate the operations of Bandlow and Morse [ 2 ] where the RSK and jeu de taquin algorithms are used to interpret the coefficients in the Schur expansion of stable and dual stable Grothendieck polynomials. The advantage is that oscillating sequences naturally form a distributive lattice, see Corollary 3.10 , so that we could be able to use the framework that have been developed by Chan, Chen, Pak and Soskin [ 4 ] to deal with correlation inequalities.




 • 
 
 We use a multivariate Reuter–Lovász–Saks type inequality on finite distributive lattices, see Theorem 4.1 , as well as
a variation of the Schur orchestra inequality by Chan, Chen, Pak and Soskin [ 4 , Theorem 5.2] , see Theorem 5.2 .




 


 
 Remark 1.8.

 
 In private communications, Swee Hong Chan pointed out that Theorem 4.1 can be implied (not immediately obviously) by [ 5 , Claim 6.3] , and Theorem 5.2 follows from [ 4 , Theorem 7.14] . Since the statement in [ 4 , Theorem 7.14] is not needed in [ 4 ] , its proof is omitted in [ 4 ] . For clarity and self-containedness, we will include proofs of Theorems 4.1 and 5.2 in this paper. 





 
 Remark 1.9.

 
 The Okounkov inequality for Schur functions was conjectured by Okounkov [ 23 ] , and proved by Lam, Postnikov and Pylyavskyy [ 16 , Theorem 4] : 

 

 
 
 s λ ( 𝐱 ) s μ ( 𝐱 ) ≤ s s ⌈ ( λ + μ ) / 2 ⌉ ( 𝐱 ) s ⌊ ( λ + μ ) / 2 ⌋ ( 𝐱 ) . s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})s_{\mu}(\mathbf{x})\leq_{s}s_{\left\lceil(\lambda+\mu)/2\right\rceil}(\mathbf{x})s_{\left\lfloor(\lambda+\mu)/2\right\rfloor}(\mathbf{x}). 
 
 
 
 Combining Theorems 1.2 and 1.3 with Speyer’s theorem [ 24 ] (see [ 4 , Theorem 3.5] ), Chan, Chen, Pak and Soskin [ 4 , Corollaries 9.5 and 10.3] obtained the Okounkov inequalities for stable and dual stable Grothendieck polynomials.
We can also prove a multivariate Okounkov inequality for hybrid Grothendieck polynomials.
The proof needs additional techniques, and will be presented in future work. 







 
 1.3 Conjectured extensions of the LPP inequality


 
 In the Schubert calculus of the flag variety, Schur polynomials arise canonically as a subclass of Schubert polynomials, see Remark 1.10 .
It is therefore natural to ask if there exists a Schubert positivity extension which includes
the classical Schur LPP inequality as a special case.
Let S n S_{n} denote the symmetric group of permutations of { 1 , 2 , … , n } \{1,2,\ldots,n\} . Write
 w = w ( 1 ) ⋯ w ( n ) ∈ S n w=w(1)\cdots w(n)\in S_{n} in one-line notation.
The
Schubert polynomials 𝔖 w ​ ( 𝐱 ) \mathfrak{S}_{w}(\mathbf{x}) , introduced by Lascoux and Schützenberger [ 18 ] , represent the Schubert classes in the cohomology ring of the flag variety.



 
 To define { 𝔖 w ​ ( 𝐱 ) : w ∈ S n } \{\mathfrak{S}_{w}(\mathbf{x})\colon w\in S_{n}\} , recall the divided difference operator ∂ i \partial_{i} acting on a polynomial f ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) f(\mathbf{x}) by letting ∂ i ( f ) = f − s i ⋅ f x i − x i + 1 , \partial_{i}(f)=\frac{f-s_{i}\cdot f}{x_{i}-x_{i+1}}, 
where s i ⋅ f s_{i}\cdot f is obtained from f f by interchanging the variables x i x_{i} and x i + 1 x_{i+1} .
For w 0 = n ⋯ 21 w_{0}=n\cdots 21 , set 𝔖 w 0 ( 𝐱 ) = x 1 n − 1 x 2 n − 2 ⋯ x n − 1 \mathfrak{S}_{w_{0}}(\mathbf{x})=x_{1}^{n-1}x_{2}^{n-2}\cdots x_{n-1} .
If w ≠ w 0 w\neq w_{0} , then there must exist an index 1 ≤ i < n 1\leq i<n such that w ⁡ ( i ) < w ⁡ ( i + 1 ) w(i)<w(i+1) . Define 𝔖 w ​ ( 𝐱 ) = ∂ i 𝔖 w ​ s i ​ ( 𝐱 ) , \mathfrak{S}_{w}(\mathbf{x})=\partial_{i}\,\mathfrak{S}_{ws_{i}}(\mathbf{x}), where w ​ s i ws_{i} is obtained from w w by swapping w ⁡ ( i ) w(i) and w ⁡ ( i + 1 ) w(i+1) .
This is independent of the choice of i i since the operators ∂ i \partial_{i} satisfy the Coxeter relations.



 
 The Schubert polynomials 𝔖 w ​ ( 𝐱 ) \mathfrak{S}_{w}(\mathbf{x}) where w ∈ S n w\in S_{n} are linearly independent, constituting a basis
of the space span ℝ { x 1 a 1 ⋯ x n a n : 0 ≤ a i ≤ n − i } \mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{x_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{a_{n}}:0\leq a_{i}\leq n-i\} . For
two polynomials f f and g g , write f ≤ 𝔖 g f\leq_{\mathfrak{S}}g 
if g − f g-f is Schubert positive:

 

 
 
 g − f ∈ ∑ w ℝ ≥ 0 ⋅ 𝔖 w . g-f\in\sum_{w}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\cdot\mathfrak{S}_{w}. 
 
 
 
 An expected Schubert LPP inequality would look like

 

 
 
 𝔖 u ( 𝐱 ) 𝔖 v ( 𝐱 ) ≤ 𝔖 𝔖 u ∨ v ( 𝐱 ) 𝔖 u ∧ v ( 𝐱 ) . \mathfrak{S}_{u}(\mathbf{x})\mathfrak{S}_{v}(\mathbf{x})\leq_{\mathfrak{S}}\mathfrak{S}_{u\vee v}(\mathbf{x})\mathfrak{S}_{u\wedge v}(\mathbf{x}). 
 
 (1.2) 
 
 
 The first task is how to define the join and meet of two permutations. To do this, we use the Lehmer code (also called inversion code) of w w :

 

 
 
 c ( w ) = ( c 1 ( w ) , … , c n ( w ) ) , c i ( w ) = # { j : i < j ≤ n , w ( i ) > w ( j ) } . c(w)=(c_{1}(w),\ldots,c_{n}(w)),\hskip 20.00003ptc_{i}(w)=\#\{j:i<j\leq n,\ w(i)>w(j)\}. 
 
 
 
 It is easily seen that 0 ≤ c i ​ ( w ) ≤ n − i 0\leq c_{i}(w)\leq n-i , and conversely every such integer vector determines
a permutation in S n S_{n} .
For u , v ∈ S n u,v\in S_{n} , define their join and
meet are permutations determined by the following Lehmer codes:

 

 
 
 c i ​ ( u ∨ v ) = max ⁡ { c i ​ ( u ) , c i ​ ( v ) } , c i ​ ( u ∧ v ) = min ⁡ { c i ​ ( u ) , c i ​ ( v ) } . c_{i}(u\vee v)=\max\{c_{i}(u),c_{i}(v)\},\hskip 20.00003ptc_{i}(u\wedge v)=\min\{c_{i}(u),c_{i}(v)\}. 
 
 
 
 Obviously, the above are indeed valid Lehmer codes of length n n , and so
 u ∨ v u\vee v and u ∧ v u\wedge v are permutations belonging to S n S_{n} .



 
 Remark 1.10.

 
 Let w w be a k k -Grassmannian, that is, it has at most one descent at position k k . It is easy to check that
 c 1 ​ ( w ) ≤ ⋯ ≤ c k ​ ( w ) c_{1}(w)\leq\cdots\leq c_{k}(w) and c i ​ ( w ) = 0 c_{i}(w)=0 for i > k i>k . Denote by λ ⁡ ( w ) = ( c k ​ ( w ) , … , c 1 ​ ( w ) ) \lambda(w)=(c_{k}(w),\ldots,c_{1}(w)) the associated partition. It is a classical fact that
 𝔖 w ​ ( 𝐱 ) \mathfrak{S}_{w}(\mathbf{x}) is the Schur polynomial s λ ⁡ ( w ) ​ ( x 1 , … , x k ) s_{\lambda(w)}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}) . Notice that when u u and v v are k k -Grassmannians, both u ∨ v u\vee v and u ∧ v u\wedge v are also k k -Grassmannians, and moreover λ ⁡ ( u ∨ v ) = λ ⁡ ( u ) ∨ λ ⁡ ( v ) \lambda(u\vee v)=\lambda(u)\vee\lambda(v) and λ ⁡ ( u ∧ v ) = λ ⁡ ( u ) ∧ λ ⁡ ( v ) \lambda(u\wedge v)=\lambda(u)\wedge\lambda(v) . Therefore, in such a case, ( 1.2 ) will reduce to the LPP inequality in Theorem 1.1 . 





 
 For general u u and v v , we could immediately find examples that violate ( 1.2 ): for instance u = 132 u=132 and v = 213 v=213 . A reasonable route is to consider permutations whose Schubert polynomials are close to Schur polynomials. Arguably, vexillary permutations are such permutations: w w is vexillary if it avoids the pattern
 2143 2143 , that is, there do not exist i < j < k < ℓ i<j<k<\ell such that w ⁡ ( j ) < w ⁡ ( i ) < w ⁡ ( ℓ ) < w ⁡ ( k ) w(j)<w(i)<w(\ell)<w(k) . This class of permutations plays an important role in the combinatorics and geometry of Schubert varieties and degeneracy loci, see for example Fulton [ 9 ] , Eriksson–Linusson [ 7 ] and Knutson–Miller–Yong [ 14 ] .



 
 Wachs’ theorem [ 27 ] identifies the Schubert polynomial of a
vexillary permutation with a flagged Schur polynomial.
Precisely, for w ∈ S n w\in S_{n} , set

 

 
 
 I i ( w ) = { j : i < j ≤ n , w ( i ) > w ( j ) } . I_{i}(w)=\{j:i<j\leq n,\ w(i)>w(j)\}. 
 
 
 
 So we have c i ​ ( w ) = # ​ I i ​ ( w ) c_{i}(w)=\#I_{i}(w) .
For every i i with c i ​ ( w ) > 0 c_{i}(w)>0 , define

 

 
 
 β i ​ ( w ) = min ⁡ I i ​ ( w ) − 1 . \beta_{i}(w)=\min I_{i}(w)-1. 
 
 
 
 Arrange the entries { c i ( w ) > 0 } \{c_{i}(w)>0\} in weakly decreasing order to get a partition λ ⁡ ( w ) = ( λ 1 ≥ ⋯ ≥ λ k ) \lambda(w)=(\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{k}) , and the values { β i ​ ( w ) : c i ​ ( w ) > 0 } \{\beta_{i}(w):c_{i}(w)>0\} in weakly increasing order to get a flag ϕ ⁡ ( w ) = ( ϕ 1 ≤ ⋯ ≤ ϕ k ) \phi(w)=(\phi_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\phi_{k}) .
When w w is vexillary, it is well known that 𝔖 w = s λ ⁡ ( w ) ϕ ⁡ ( w ) \mathfrak{S}_{w}=s_{\lambda(w)}^{\phi(w)} ,
the flagged Schur polynomial of shape λ ⁡ ( w ) \lambda(w) and flagged by ϕ ⁡ ( w ) \phi(w) [ 27 ] .



 
 Remark 1.11.

 
 A Schubert polynomial 𝔖 w \mathfrak{S}_{w} is a flagged Schur polynomial if and only if w w is vexillary [ 18 ] . However, not every flagged Schur polynomial can be realized as a Schubert polynomial. 





 
 For two finite sequences a = ( a 1 , … , a r ) a=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}) and
 b = ( b 1 , … , b s ) b=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{s}) , write a ⪯ b a\preceq b 
if a a is a prefix of b b , that is, r ≤ s r\leq s and a i = b i a_{i}=b_{i} for
 1 ≤ i ≤ r 1\leq i\leq r .



 
 Conjecture 1.12 (vexillary Schubert LPP inequality) .

 
 Let u , v ∈ S n u,v\in S_{n} be vexillary permutations. Suppose that their Wachs
flags satisfy ϕ ⁡ ( u ) ⪯ ϕ ⁡ ( v ) \phi(u)\preceq\phi(v) .
Then 

 

 
 
 𝔖 u ( 𝐱 ) 𝔖 v ( 𝐱 ) ≤ 𝔖 𝔖 u ∨ v ( 𝐱 ) 𝔖 u ∧ v ( 𝐱 ) . \mathfrak{S}_{u}(\mathbf{x})\mathfrak{S}_{v}(\mathbf{x})\leq_{\mathfrak{S}}\mathfrak{S}_{u\vee v}(\mathbf{x})\mathfrak{S}_{u\wedge v}(\mathbf{x}). 
 
 
 




 
 The above conjecture has been verified for n ≤ 8 n\leq 8 .



 
 Remark 1.13.

 
 Notice that Grassmannian permutations are vexillary permutations. The Wachs flag of a k k -Grassmannian has all entries equal to k k . Hence when both u u and v v are k k -Grassmannians, Conjecture 1.12 would specialize to Theorem 1.1 . 





 
 We give an example to illustrate Conjecture 1.12 .
Take two vexillary permutations u = 5712364 u=5712364 and v = 3514672 v=3514672 in S 7 S_{7} , which have Lehmer codes

 

 
 
 c ⁡ ( u ) = ( 4 , 5 , 0 , 0 , 0 , 1 , 0 ) , c ⁡ ( v ) = ( 2 , 3 , 0 , 1 , 1 , 1 , 0 ) , c(u)=(4,5,0,0,0,1,0),\ \ \ \ \ c(v)=(2,3,0,1,1,1,0), 
 
 
 
 and Wachs flags

 

 
 
 ϕ ⁡ ( u ) = ( 2 , 2 , 6 ) ⪯ ϕ ⁡ ( v ) = ( 2 , 2 , 6 , 6 , 6 ) . \phi(u)=(2,2,6)\,\preceq\,\phi(v)=(2,2,6,6,6). 
 
 
 
 So we have
 u ∨ v = 5713462 u\vee v=5713462 and u ∧ v = 3512476 u\wedge v=3512476 .
Note that u ∧ v u\wedge v has the pattern 2143 2143 , and thus is no longer vexillary. By direct computation,

 

 
 
 𝔖 u ∨ v ​ 𝔖 u ∧ v − 𝔖 u ​ 𝔖 v = 2 ​ 𝔖 ( 7 , 8 , 0 , 1 , 1 , 1 ) + 𝔖 ( 6 , 9 , 0 , 1 , 1 , 1 ) , \mathfrak{S}_{u\vee v}\mathfrak{S}_{u\wedge v}-\mathfrak{S}_{u}\mathfrak{S}_{v}=2\,\mathfrak{S}_{(7,8,0,1,1,1)}+\mathfrak{S}_{(6,9,0,1,1,1)}, 
 
 
 
 where, on the right-hand side, we use the Lehmer codes to indicate permutations (the former is a permutation in S 10 S_{10} , and the latter is in S 11 S_{11} ).



 
 We also formulate an equivariant version of Conjecture 1.12 . The double Schubert polynomials 𝔖 w ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐲 ) \mathfrak{S}_{w}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y}) represent the Schubert classes in the torus-equivariant cohomology of the flag variety. In the equivariant case, we set

 

 
 
 𝔖 w 0 ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐲 ) = ∏ i + j ≤ n ( x i − y j ) . \mathfrak{S}_{w_{0}}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y})=\prod_{i+j\leq n}(x_{i}-y_{j}). 
 
 
 
 If w ≠ w 0 w\neq w_{0} , let 𝔖 w ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐲 ) = ∂ i 𝔖 w ​ s i ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐲 ) \mathfrak{S}_{w}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y})=\partial_{i}\,\mathfrak{S}_{ws_{i}}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y}) where w ⁡ ( i ) < w ⁡ ( i + 1 ) w(i)<w(i+1) . Here ∂ i \partial_{i} acts on the 𝐱 \mathbf{x} variables.
It is clear that 𝔖 w ​ ( 𝐱 ) = 𝔖 w ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐲 ) | y i = 0 \mathfrak{S}_{w}(\mathbf{x})=\mathfrak{S}_{w}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y})|_{y_{i}=0} .



 
 For i ≥ 1 i\geq 1 , write
 α i = y i + 1 − y i \alpha_{i}=y_{i+1}-y_{i} . By Graham’s positivity theorem [ 12 ] , the structure constants c u , v w ​ ( 𝐲 ) c^{w}_{u,v}(\mathbf{y}) in the expansion of 𝔖 u ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐲 ) ​ 𝔖 v ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐲 ) \mathfrak{S}_{u}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y})\mathfrak{S}_{v}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y}) satisfies

 

 
 
 𝔖 u ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐲 ) ​ 𝔖 v ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐲 ) = ∑ w c u , v w ​ ( 𝐲 ) ​ 𝔖 w ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐲 ) , where  c u , v w ​ ( 𝐲 ) ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ [ α 1 , α 2 , … ] . \mathfrak{S}_{u}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y})\mathfrak{S}_{v}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y})=\sum_{w}c^{w}_{u,v}(\mathbf{y})\mathfrak{S}_{w}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y}),\ \ \text{where $c^{w}_{u,v}(\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots]$}. 
 
 
 
 This motivates the equivariant Schubert positivity: write f ≤ 𝔖 equiv g f\leq_{\mathfrak{S}}^{\text{equiv}}g 
if

 

 
 
 g − f ∈ ∑ w ℝ ≥ 0 ​ [ α 1 , α 2 , … ] ⋅ 𝔖 w ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐲 ) . g-f\in\sum_{w}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots]\cdot\mathfrak{S}_{w}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y}). 
 
 
 


 
 Conjecture 1.14 (vexillary equivariant Schubert LPP inequality) .

 
 Let u , v ∈ S n u,v\in S_{n} be vexillary permutations. Suppose that their Wachs
flags satisfy ϕ ⁡ ( u ) ⪯ ϕ ⁡ ( v ) \phi(u)\preceq\phi(v) .
Then 

 

 
 
 𝔖 u ( 𝐱 ; 𝐲 ) 𝔖 v ( 𝐱 ; 𝐲 ) ≤ 𝔖 equiv 𝔖 u ∨ v ( 𝐱 ; 𝐲 ) 𝔖 u ∧ v ( 𝐱 ; 𝐲 ) . \mathfrak{S}_{u}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y})\mathfrak{S}_{v}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y})\leq_{\mathfrak{S}}^{\mathrm{equiv}}\mathfrak{S}_{u\vee v}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y})\mathfrak{S}_{u\wedge v}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y}). 
 
 
 
 This conjecture will specialize to Conjecture 1.12 by taking 𝐲 = 𝟎 \mathbf{y}=\mathbf{0} . 





 
 Because of the computational complexity of double Schubert polynomials, we tested Conjecture 1.14 randomly for vexillary permutations in S n S_{n} for n = 9 n=9 .



 
 When restricting w w to a k k -Grassmannian, 𝔖 w ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐲 ) \mathfrak{S}_{w}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y}) is equal to the double Schur polynomial s λ ⁡ ( w ) ​ ( 𝐱 k , 𝐲 ) s_{\lambda(w)}(\mathbf{x}_{k};\mathbf{y}) , where 𝐱 k = ( x 1 , … , x k ) \mathbf{x}_{k}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}) , which is also called the factorial Schur polynomial, see for example [ 15 , 22 ] . In the Grassmannian case, Conjecture 1.14 leads to an equivariant extension of Theorem 1.1 .



 
 Conjecture 1.15 (equivariant Schur LPP inequality) .

 
 For partitions λ \lambda and μ \mu , we have 

 

 
 
 s λ ( 𝐱 k ; 𝐲 ) s μ ( 𝐱 k ; 𝐲 ) ≤ s equiv s λ ∨ μ ( 𝐱 k ; 𝐲 ) s λ ∧ μ ( 𝐱 k ; 𝐲 ) . s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}_{k};\mathbf{y})s_{\mu}(\mathbf{x}_{k};\mathbf{y})\leq_{s}^{\mathrm{{equiv}}}s_{\lambda\vee\mu}(\mathbf{x}_{k};\mathbf{y})s_{\lambda\wedge\mu}(\mathbf{x}_{k};\mathbf{y}). 
 
 
 
 This conjecture will specialize to the classical LPP inequality by taking 𝐲 = 𝟎 \mathbf{y}=\mathbf{0} . 





 
 Conjecture 1.15 has been checked
for all Grassmannians in S n S_{n} with n n up to 7 7 , and randomly for Grassmannians in S n S_{n} with n = 10 n=10 .



 
 We lastly turn to the Grothendieck setting. As the K-theory counterpart, Grothendieck polynomials 𝔊 w ​ ( 𝐱 ) \mathfrak{G}_{w}(\mathbf{x}) are polynomial representatives of Schubert classes in the Grothendieck ring of the flag variety [ 19 ] . We shall use the β \beta -version 𝔊 w ( β ) ​ ( 𝐱 ) \mathfrak{G}_{w}^{(\beta)}(\mathbf{x}) defined by Fomin and Kirillov [ 8 ] , which specializes to 𝔖 w ​ ( 𝐱 ) \mathfrak{S}_{w}(\mathbf{x}) and 𝔊 w ​ ( 𝐱 ) \mathfrak{G}_{w}(\mathbf{x}) by taking β = 0 \beta=0 and β = − 1 \beta=-1 , respectively.



 
 The β \beta -Grothendieck polynomials are defined in the same way as Schubert polynomials, except that we replace ∂ i \partial_{i} by the operator
 π i ( β ) ​ ( f ) = ∂ i ( ( 1 + β ​ x i + 1 ) ​ f ) \pi_{i}^{(\beta)}(f)=\partial_{i}((1+\beta x_{i+1})f) .
Now, for two polynomials f f and g g , write f ≤ 𝔊 ( β ) g f\leq_{\mathfrak{G}^{(\beta)}}g 
if

 

 
 
 g − f ∈ ∑ w ℝ ≥ 0 ​ [ β ] ⋅ 𝔊 w ( β ) . g-f\in\sum_{w}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}[\beta]\cdot\mathfrak{G}_{w}^{(\beta)}. 
 
 
 


 
 The following K-theory analogue of Conjecture 1.12 has been checked for n ≤ 7 n\leq 7 .



 
 Conjecture 1.16 (vexillary Grothendieck LPP inequality) .

 
 Let u , v ∈ S n u,v\in S_{n} be vexillary permutations. Suppose that their Wachs
flags satisfy ϕ ⁡ ( u ) ⪯ ϕ ⁡ ( v ) \phi(u)\preceq\phi(v) .
Then 

 

 
 
 𝔊 u ( β ) ( 𝐱 ) 𝔊 v ( β ) ( 𝐱 ) ≤ 𝔊 ( β ) 𝔊 u ∨ v ( β ) ( 𝐱 ) 𝔊 u ∧ v ( β ) ( 𝐱 ) . \mathfrak{G}_{u}^{(\beta)}(\mathbf{x})\mathfrak{G}_{v}^{(\beta)}(\mathbf{x})\leq_{\mathfrak{G}^{(\beta)}}\mathfrak{G}^{(\beta)}_{u\vee v}(\mathbf{x})\mathfrak{G}^{(\beta)}_{u\wedge v}(\mathbf{x}). 
 
 
 
 This conjecture will specialize to Conjecture 1.12 by taking β = 0 \beta=0 . 





 
 Similarly to the Schubert case, when w w is a vexillay permutation, 𝔊 w ( β ) ​ ( 𝐱 ) \mathfrak{G}_{w}^{(\beta)}(\mathbf{x}) can be constructed by flagged set-valued tableaux, see for example [ 14 ] .
In particular, for w w 
a k k -Grassmannian, its Grothendieck polynomial (for β = 1 \beta=1 ) is equal to G ~ λ ⁡ ( w ) ​ ( 𝐱 k ) \widetilde{G}_{\lambda(w)}(\mathbf{x}_{k}) . Hence when restricted to k k -Grassmannians, Conjecture 1.16 reduces to a statement for stable Grothendieck polynomials, as previously predicted by Thomas and Yong [ 26 , Conjecture 9.2] (see also Mihalcea [ 21 ] ) which remains open.



 
 Conjecture 1.17 ( [ 26 , Conjecture 9.2] ) .

 
 For partitions λ \lambda and μ \mu , we have 

 

 
 
 G ~ λ ( 𝐱 ) G ~ μ ( 𝐱 ) ≤ G ~ G ~ λ ∨ μ ( 𝐱 ) G ~ λ ∧ μ ( 𝐱 ) . \widetilde{G}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})\widetilde{G}_{\mu}(\mathbf{x})\leq_{\widetilde{G}}\widetilde{G}_{\lambda\vee\mu}(\mathbf{x})\widetilde{G}_{\lambda\wedge\mu}(\mathbf{x}). 
 
 
 




 
 The double Grothendieck polynomials are the K-theory analogue of double Schubert polynomials. The structure constants in this setting are positive in the sense of Anderson, Griffeth and Miller [ 1 ] . The numerical data indicate that Conjectures 1.16 and 1.17 might also possess an equivariant Grothendieck LPP positivity.



 
 The structure of this paper is as follows. In Section 2 , we lay out necessary definitions and notation for this paper. In Section 3 , we introduce the notion of oscillating sequences which is the starting point of our proof for Theorem 1.5 . In particular, oscillating sequences form a distributive lattice.
Sections 4 and 5 are devoted to a multivariate Reuter–Lovász–Saks type inequality and the Schur orchestra inequality, respectively.
Collecting the results in Sections 3 , 4 and 5 , we complete the proof of Theorem 1.5 in Section 6 .

 2 Preliminaries


 
 In this section, we shall collect some terminology and notation. Some basic properties that we require will also be mentioned.
In particular, we refer to [ 10 , §1.1] and [ 25 , Chapter 7] for the theory of symmetric functions.



 
 2.1 Partition and Young lattice


 
 A partition is a weakly decreasing sequence λ = ( λ 1 , λ 2 , … ) \lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots) of nonnegative integers with only finitely many nonzero parts. Denote by | λ | = ∑ i λ i |\lambda|=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i} the size of λ \lambda , and by ℓ ⁡ ( λ ) \ell(\lambda) the number of nonzero parts of λ \lambda .
The Young diagram of λ \lambda is a left-justified array of boxes with λ i \lambda_{i} boxes in row i i . Let ( i , j ) (i,j) represent the box in row i i and column j j . Here the row (resp., column) indices are counted from top to bottom (resp., from left to right). Let 𝒫 \mathcal{P} represent the set of all partitions. We use μ ⊆ λ \mu\subseteq\lambda to mean that the Young diagram of μ \mu is contained in that of λ \lambda , namely, λ i ≥ μ i \lambda_{i}\geq\mu_{i} for all i ≥ 1 i\geq 1 . Under the inclusion ⊆ \subseteq of partitions, 𝒫 \mathcal{P} forms a distributive poset, called the Young lattice. Specifically, for two partitions λ \lambda and μ \mu , their join λ ∨ μ \lambda\vee\mu and meet λ ∧ μ \lambda\wedge\mu are partitions satisfying

 

 
 
 ( λ ∨ μ ) i = max ⁡ { λ i , μ i } , ( λ ∧ μ ) i = min ⁡ { λ i , μ i } . (\lambda\vee\mu)_{i}=\max\{\lambda_{i},\mu_{i}\},\hskip 20.00003pt(\lambda\wedge\mu)_{i}=\min\{\lambda_{i},\mu_{i}\}. 
 
 
 


 
 For μ ⊆ λ \mu\subseteq\lambda , the skew diagram λ / μ \lambda/\mu is obtained from the diagram λ \lambda by removing the boxes in μ \mu . We say λ / μ \lambda/\mu is a horizontal (resp., vertical ) strip if there are no two boxes lying in the same column (resp., row).
Here we allow a horizontal or vertical strip to be empty (namely, in the case λ = μ \lambda=\mu ).





 
 2.2  Schur and flagged Schur function


 
 Let λ / μ \lambda/\mu be a skew shape. A semistandard Young tableau of shape λ / μ \lambda/\mu is a filling
 T : λ / μ ⟶ ℤ > 0 T\colon\lambda/\mu\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{>0} of the boxes of λ / μ \lambda/\mu with positive integers,
such that the entries are weakly increasing from left to right along each row and strictly increasing from top to bottom along each column. In other words, whenever the corresponding boxes belong to λ / μ \lambda/\mu , we have

 

 
 
 T ⁡ ( i , j ) ≤ T ⁡ ( i , j + 1 ) , T ⁡ ( i , j ) < T ⁡ ( i + 1 , j ) . T(i,j)\leq T(i,j+1),\hskip 20.00003ptT(i,j)<T(i+1,j). 
 
 
 


 
 Denote by SSYT ⁡ ( λ / μ ) \SSYT(\lambda/\mu) the set of all semistandard tableaux of shape λ / μ \lambda/\mu . For T ∈ SSYT ⁡ ( λ / μ ) T\in\SSYT(\lambda/\mu) , its weight is the sequence wt ⁡ ( T ) = ( wt 1 ⁡ ( T ) , wt 2 ⁡ ( T ) , … ) , \wt(T)=(\wt_{1}(T),\wt_{2}(T),\ldots), where wt i ⁡ ( T ) \wt_{i}(T) is the number of entries of T T equal to i i . Write

 

 
 
 𝐱 wt ⁡ ( T ) = ∏ i ≥ 1 x i wt i ⁡ ( T ) . \mathbf{x}^{\wt(T)}=\prod_{i\geq 1}x_{i}^{\wt_{i}(T)}. 
 
 
 
 The Schur function indexed by skew shape λ / μ \lambda/\mu is defined by

 

 
 
 s λ / μ ​ ( 𝐱 ) = ∑ T ∈ SSYT ⁡ ( λ / μ ) 𝐱 wt ⁡ ( T ) . s_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{T\in\SSYT(\lambda/\mu)}\mathbf{x}^{\wt(T)}. 
 
 
 
 In the straight shape case when μ = ∅ \mu=\emptyset , we write s λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}) instead of s λ / ∅ ​ ( 𝐱 ) s_{\lambda/\emptyset}(\mathbf{x}) . If we use a finite number of variables 𝐱 n = ( x 1 , … , x n ) \mathbf{x}_{n}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}) , then we are given a Schur polynomial.
It is a classical result that the set of Schur functions forms a linear basis of the ring of symmetric functions.



 
 The flagged Schur polynomials are generalizations of Schur polynomials. A flag means
a weakly increasing sequence ϕ = ( ϕ 1 , ϕ 2 , … , ϕ r ) \phi=(\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\ldots,\phi_{r}) of positive integers.
Suppose that λ / μ \lambda/\mu has at most r r rows. A flagged semistandard tableau of shape λ / μ \lambda/\mu and with flag ϕ \phi is a semistandard tableau of shape λ / μ \lambda/\mu such that every entry in row i i cannot exceed ϕ i \phi_{i} .
Denote the set of such tableaux by SSYT ⁡ ( λ / μ , ϕ ) \SSYT(\lambda/\mu,{\phi}) . The corresponding flagged Schur function is

 

 
 
 s λ / μ ϕ ​ ( 𝐱 ) = ∑ T ∈ SSYT ⁡ ( λ / μ , ϕ ) 𝐱 wt ⁡ ( T ) . s_{\lambda/\mu}^{\phi}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{T\in\SSYT(\lambda/\mu,{\phi})}\mathbf{x}^{\wt(T)}. 
 
 
 




 
 2.3 Stable and dual stable Grothendieck polynomial


 
 Stable Grothendieck polynomials G λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) G_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}) and their duals g λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) g_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}) are nonhomogeneous generalizations of Schur functions.
We first recall the set-valued tableau construction of G λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) G_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}) pioneered by Buch [ 3 ] . For two nonempty finite sets A , B ⊂ ℤ > 0 A,B\subset\mathbb{Z}_{>0} , we write A ≤ B A\leq B (resp., A < B A<B ) to mean that max ⁡ A ≤ min ⁡ B \max A\leq\min B (resp., max ⁡ A < min ⁡ B \max A<\min B ).
A set-valued tableau of shape λ / μ \lambda/\mu is a filling T T of each box of λ / μ \lambda/\mu with a nonempty finite subset of ℤ > 0 \mathbb{Z}_{>0} such that the sets are weakly increasing along each row, and strictly increasing down each column.
Let SVT ⁡ ( λ / μ ) \SVT(\lambda/\mu) be the set of all set-valued tableaux of shape λ / μ \lambda/\mu .



 
 For T ∈ SVT ⁡ ( λ / μ ) T\in\SVT(\lambda/\mu) , define | T | = ∑ ( i , j ) ∈ λ / μ # ​ T ​ ( i , j ) |T|=\sum_{(i,j)\in\lambda/\mu}\#T(i,j) . Its weight is wt ⁡ ( T ) = ( wt 1 ⁡ ( T ) , wt 2 ⁡ ( T ) , … ) , \wt(T)=(\wt_{1}(T),\wt_{2}(T),\ldots), where wt i ⁡ ( T ) \wt_{i}(T) is the total number of occurrences of i i appearing in T T . The stable Grothendieck polynomial of skew shape λ / μ \lambda/\mu is defined by

 

 
 
 G λ / μ ​ ( 𝐱 ) = ∑ T ∈ SVT ⁡ ( λ / μ ) ( − 1 ) | T | − | λ / μ | ​ 𝐱 wt ⁡ ( T ) . G_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{T\in\SVT(\lambda/\mu)}(-1)^{|T|-|\lambda/\mu|}\mathbf{x}^{\wt(T)}. 
 
 
 
 It is easy to see that the Schur function s λ s_{\lambda} is equal to the lowest-degree homogeneous component of G λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) G_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}) .



 
 The Hall inner product on symmetric functions is defined by setting < s λ , s μ > = δ λ , μ <s_{\lambda},s_{\mu}>=\delta_{\lambda,\mu} . As the dual basis of G λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) G_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}) ,
the dual stable Grothendieck polynomials g λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) g_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}) were extensively studied by Lam and Pylyavskyy [ 17 ] . Unlike G λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) G_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}) , the dual g λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) g_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}) is combinatorially constructed in terms of reverse plane partitions. Recall that a reverse plane partition of shape λ / μ \lambda/\mu is a filling T : λ / μ ⟶ ℤ > 0 T\colon\lambda/\mu\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{>0} of the boxes in λ / μ \lambda/\mu such that the entries are weakly increasing along each row and each column. Denote by RPP ⁡ ( λ / μ ) \RPP(\lambda/\mu) the set of reverse plane partitions of shape λ / μ \lambda/\mu .



 
 For T ∈ RPP ⁡ ( λ / μ ) T\in\RPP(\lambda/\mu) , the irredundant content vector ircont ⁡ ( T ) = ( r 1 , r 2 , … ) \mathrm{ircont}(T)=(r_{1},r_{2},\ldots) is defined by letting r i r_{i} be the number of columns contains at least one i i . Define

 

 
 
 g λ / μ ​ ( 𝐱 ) = ∑ T ∈ RPP ⁡ ( λ / μ ) 𝐱 ircont ⁡ ( T ) . g_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{T\in\RPP(\lambda/\mu)}\mathbf{x}^{\mathrm{ircont}(T)}. 
 
 
 
 It follows from the definition that the highest-degree homogeneous component of g λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) g_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}) is the Schur function s λ s_{\lambda} .



 
 Lam and Pylyavskyy [ 17 ] showed that < G λ , g μ > = δ λ , μ <G_{\lambda},g_{\mu}>=\delta_{\lambda,\mu} . Importantly, this relation yields an alternative way to compute the structure constants in the K-theory of Grassmannians, see also Li, Morse and Shields [ 20 ] .



 
 The expansions of G λ G_{\lambda} and g λ g_{\lambda} in the basis of Schur functions have been given in [ 3 ] and [ 17 ] , respectively, see also Bandlow and Morse [ 2 ] . The coefficients appearing in the expansion have explicit combinatorial formulas in terms of certain kinds of tableaux. Such formulas are crucial in the proofs of Theorems 1.2 and 1.3 , as given by
Chan, Chen, Pak and Soskin [ 4 ] .





 
 2.4 Hybrid Grothendieck polynomial


 
 The hybrid Grothendieck polynomials H λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) H_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}) , which are defined based on set-valued reverse plane partitions, provide a unified platform to investigate stable and dual stable Grothendieck polynomials.
A set-valued reverse plane partition of shape λ / μ \lambda/\mu is a filling of every box of λ / μ \lambda/\mu with a nonempty finite set of positive integers, such that the sets are weakly increasing along each row and each column. See Figure 1 for an illustration. The set of such fillings is denoted by SVRPP ⁡ ( λ / μ ) \SVRPP(\lambda/\mu) .

 23 23 
     
 
 
 
 
 
 
 12 12 
     
 
 
 
 
 
 34 34 
     
 
 
 
 
 
 
 23 23 
     
 
 
 
 
 
 5 5 
     
 
 
 
 
 
 
 35 35 
     
 
 
 

 
 
 Figure 1: A set-valued reverse plane
partition. Here we have omitted the braces { } \{\ \} for sets as well as the commas between numbers. 
 
 
 For T ∈ SVRPP ⁡ ( λ / μ ) T\in\SVRPP(\lambda/\mu) and a box ( i , j ) ∈ λ / μ (i,j)\in\lambda/\mu , let T ⁡ ( i , j ) T(i,j) be the set in ( i , j ) (i,j) . To define H λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) H_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}) , we need the following statistics:

 
 • 
 
 the irredundant content vector ircont ⁡ ( T ) = ( r 1 , r 2 , … ) \mathrm{ircont}(T)=(r_{1},r_{2},\ldots) , where r i r_{i} is the number of columns containing a set which contains i i ;




 • 
 
 the column equality vector ceq ⁡ ( T ) = ( c 1 , c 2 , … ) \ceq(T)=(c_{1},c_{2},\ldots) , where c i c_{i} is the number of boxes ( i , j ) (i,j) in row i i such that ( i + 1 , j ) ∈ λ / μ (i+1,j)\in\lambda/\mu and max ⁡ T ⁡ ( i , j ) = min ⁡ T ⁡ ( i + 1 , j ) \max T(i,j)=\min T(i+1,j) ;




 • 
 
 the excess vector ex ⁡ ( T ) = ( e 1 , e 2 , … ) \ex(T)=(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots) , where

 

 
 
 e i = ∑ ( i , j ) ∈ λ / μ ( # ​ T ​ ( i , j ) − 1 ) . e_{i}=\sum_{(i,j)\in\lambda/\mu}\bigl(\#T(i,j)-1\bigr). 
 
 
 



 
 For the set-valued reverse plane partition in Figure 1 , we have

 

 
 
 ircont ⁡ ( T ) = ( 3 , 4 , 4 , 2 , 3 ) , ceq ⁡ ( T ) = ( 1 , 1 , 2 , 1 ) , ex ⁡ ( T ) = ( 0 , 2 , 3 , 3 , 1 ) . \displaystyle\mathrm{ircont}(T)=(3,4,4,2,3),\ \ \ \mathrm{ceq}(T)=(1,1,2,1),\ \ \ \mathrm{ex}(T)=(0,2,3,3,1). 
 
 
 


 
 The hybrid Grothendieck polynomial of shape λ / μ \lambda/\mu is

 

 
 
 H λ / μ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) = ∑ T ∈ SVRPP ⁡ ( λ / μ ) 𝐱 ircont ⁡ ( T ) ​ 𝐭 ceq ⁡ ( T ) ​ 𝐰 ex ⁡ ( T ) . H_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w})=\sum_{T\in\SVRPP(\lambda/\mu)}\mathbf{x}^{\mathrm{ircont}(T)}\mathbf{t}^{\ceq(T)}\mathbf{w}^{\ex(T)}. 
 
 
 
 We emphasize that 𝐱 = ( x 1 , x 2 , … ) \mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots) , 𝐭 = ( t 1 , t 2 , … ) \mathbf{t}=(t_{1},t_{2},\ldots) and 𝐰 = ( w 1 , w 2 , … ) \mathbf{w}=(w_{1},w_{2},\ldots) are countable sequences of variables, and 𝐲 α = y 1 α 1 y 2 α 2 ⋯ \mathbf{y}^{\alpha}=y_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}y_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}\cdots for a weak composition α \alpha .



 
 As aforementioned in Remark 1.4 , hybrid Grothendieck polynomials unify (refined) stable and dual stable Grothendieck polynomials. If setting all t i = 0 t_{i}=0 , then H λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝟎 , 𝐰 ) = G λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐰 ) {H}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{0},\mathbf{w})=G_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{w}) is the refined stable Grothendieck polynomial due to Chan and Pflueger [ 6 ] . If further setting all w i = − 1 w_{i}=-1 , then H λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝟎 , − 𝟏 ) {H}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{0},\mathbf{-1}) is G λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) G_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}) . We also have H λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝟎 , 𝟏 ) = G ~ λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) {H}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1})=\widetilde{G}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}) .
On the other hand, if setting all w i = 0 w_{i}=0 , then H λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐭 , 𝟎 ) = g λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐭 ) {H}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t},\mathbf{0})=g_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t}) is the refined dual stable Grothendieck polynomial introduced by Galashin, Grinberg and Liu [ 11 ] . If further setting all t i = 1 t_{i}=1 , then H λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝟏 , 𝟎 ) {H}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{1},\mathbf{0}) becomes g λ ​ ( 𝐱 ) g_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}) .



 
 It was shown in [ 13 , Theorem 1.2] 
that H λ / μ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) H_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}) is indeed symmetric in 𝐱 \mathbf{x} . Moreover, H λ / μ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) H_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}) is Schur positive. Using the crystal basis method, the coefficients K λ , μ ​ ( 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) K_{\lambda,\mu}(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}) in the Schur expansion

 

 
 
 H λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) = ∑ μ K λ , μ ​ ( 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) ​ s μ ​ ( 𝐱 ) H_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w})=\sum_{\mu}K_{\lambda,\mu}(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w})s_{\mu}(\mathbf{x}) 
 
 (2.1) 
 
 
 can be formulated in terms of set-valued reverse plane partitions satisfying the lattice word condition [ 13 , Theorem 1.3] . Note that [ 13 , Theorem 1.3] is valid for general skew shapes. This paper only concerns the straight shape situation.



 
 For our purpose to prove Theorem 1.5 , the formula of
 K λ , μ ​ ( 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) K_{\lambda,\mu}(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}) given in [ 13 , Theorem 1.3] is not available, because of the absence of a distributive lattice structure. To address this problem, inspired by the work of Bandlow and Morse [ 2 ] , we design a dilation–contraction algorithm on set-valued reverse plane partitions to give a new formula for K λ , μ ​ ( 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) K_{\lambda,\mu}(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}) , see Section 3 .





 
 2.5 RSK algorithm


 
 The RSK algorithm inserts a positive integer x x to a semistandard tableau T T of straight shape, yielding a new semistandard tableau.
The algorithm
is described in a recursive procedure.
First, insert x x into the first row of T T . If all entries in the first row are
less than or equal to x x , then place x x in a new box at the end of first row
and stop. Otherwise, let y y be the smallest entry in the first row satisfying
 y > x y>x . Replace y y by x x and insert the bumped entry y y into the
second row. Repeating the same procedure, the row insertion algorithm eventually terminates, giving rise to a new semistandard tableau, denoted
 T ← x T\leftarrow x . Note that the shape of T ← x T\leftarrow x has one more extra corner, denoted B B , than the shape of T T .
The RSK algorithm is reversible in the sense that one can uniquely recover T T and x x from T ← x T\leftarrow x and the position of the extra corner box B B .
The collection of boxes where an entry is bumped, together with the corner box where the last bumped entry lands, is called the bumping route.
Figure 2 gives an illustration of the RSK insertion for x = 3 x=3 , where the boxes on the bumping route are shaded.

 Figure 2: An illustration of the RSK algorithm. 
 
 
 The following property is not hard to justify, see [ 10 , Chapter 1] .



 
 Proposition 2.1.

 
 Let T T be a semistandard tableau of shape λ \lambda , and let 

 

 
 
 U = ( ( ( T ← x 1 ) ← x 2 ) ⋯ ) ← x p U=(((T\leftarrow x_{1})\leftarrow x_{2})\cdots)\leftarrow x_{p} 
 
 
 
 be obtained by successively inserting
 x 1 , … , x p x_{1},\ldots,x_{p} . Let μ \mu be the shape of U U .
If x 1 > x 2 > ⋯ > x p x_{1}>x_{2}>\cdots>x_{p} ,
then μ / λ \mu/\lambda forms a vertical strip.
Conversely, suppose that U U is a semistandard tableau of shape μ \mu , and that λ \lambda is a partition in μ \mu such that μ / λ \mu/\lambda has p p boxes. If μ / λ \mu/\lambda is a vertical strip, then there exist a unique semistandard tableau T T of shape λ \lambda and a unique sequence x 1 > x 2 > ⋯ > x p x_{1}>x_{2}>\cdots>x_{p} 
such that 

 

 
 
 U = ( ( ( T ← x 1 ) ← x 2 ) ⋯ ) ← x p . U=(((T\leftarrow x_{1})\leftarrow x_{2})\cdots)\leftarrow x_{p}. 
 
 
 






 
 2.6 Jeu de taquin algorithm


 
 Let λ / μ \lambda/\mu be a skew diagram. An inside corner of λ / μ \lambda/\mu 
is a box which may be removed from μ \mu while still leaving a valid Young diagram, while an outside corner of λ / μ \lambda/\mu is a box which
may be removed from λ \lambda while still leaving a Young diagram. In Figure 3 , the inside and outside corners are marked with ∘ \circ and × \times , respectively.



 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
    
 
 
 
 
 
 × {\times} 
    
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
    
 
 
 
 
 
 × {\times} 
    
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
    
 
 
 
 
 
 
    
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
    
 
 
 
 
 
 × {\times} 
    
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 3: Inside and outside corners. 
 
 
 Let S S be a semistandard tableau of shape λ / μ \lambda/\mu . Suppose that C C is an inside
corner of λ / μ \lambda/\mu . Regard C C as an empty box. The
 jeu de taquin (jdt) slide starting from C C is defined as follows.
At each step, compare the entries in the two boxes immediately to the right
and immediately below the empty box.
Move the smaller of these two entries into the empty cell. If only one of the two
neighbors exists, move that entry into the empty cell. If both neighbors exist and
have equal entries, move the lower entry into the empty cell. The empty box has now moved
to the position of the entry just moved.
This process is repeated until the empty box reaches an outside corner, yielding a new semistandard tableau.
The jdt slide is also reversible. Suppose that S ′ S^{\prime} is the semistandard tableau obtained after a jdt slide, and
that the outside corner B B removed at the end of the slide is specified. Replace B B by
an empty box. One can reverse the slide procedure to recover the original tableau S S .
For example, in Figure 4 , we apply the jdt algorithm to the inner box ( 2 , 2 ) (2,2) where the black boxes constitute the slide path.


 
 
 Figure 4: An illustration of the jdt slide. 
 




 
 3 Oscillating sequences


 
 In this section, we introduce oscillating sequences to give a new combinatorial formula for the coefficients K λ , μ ​ ( 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) K_{\lambda,\mu}(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}) appearing in the expansion in ( 2.1 ). The construction is based on the RSK and jeu de taquin algorithms. As a key property that we need,
oscillating sequences admit a distributive lattice structure.



 
 3.1 Oscillating sequences


 
 Recall that a skew shape λ / μ \lambda/\mu is a horizontal (resp., vertical) strip if there are no two boxes lying in the same column (resp., row). Here we allow a horizontal or vertical strip to be empty.
A pair of partitions ( λ , μ ) (\lambda,\mu) is called a k k -falling pair if

 
 (1) 
 
 λ ⊇ μ \lambda\supseteq\mu and λ i = μ i \lambda_{i}=\mu_{i} for all i ≤ k i\leq k ,




 (2) 
 
 λ / μ \lambda/\mu is a horizontal strip.




 
 Dually, we call a pair of partitions ( λ , μ ) (\lambda,\mu) a k k -rising pair if

 
 (1’) 
 
 λ ⊆ μ \lambda\subseteq\mu and λ i = μ i \lambda_{i}=\mu_{i} for all i ≤ k i\leq k ,




 (2’) 
 
 μ / λ \mu/\lambda is a vertical strip.




 


 
 Definition 3.1.

 
 Let λ \lambda and μ \mu be partitions. For l ≥ 1 l\geq 1 , an l l -oscillating sequence from λ \lambda to μ \mu is a sequence S = ( λ 0 , λ 1 , … , λ 2 ​ l − 1 ) S=(\lambda^{0},\lambda^{1},\ldots,\lambda^{2l-1}) of partitions
such that 

 
 (1) 
 
 λ 0 = λ \lambda^{0}=\lambda and λ 2 ​ l − 1 = μ \lambda^{2l-1}=\mu ; 




 (2) 
 
 For each 1 ≤ i ≤ l 1\leq i\leq l , the pair ( λ 2 ​ i − 2 , λ 2 ​ i − 1 ) (\lambda^{2i-2},\lambda^{2i-1}) is an ( l − i + 1 ) (l-i+1) -rising pair, and for each 1 ≤ j ≤ l − 1 1\leq j\leq l-1 , the pair ( λ 2 ​ j − 1 , λ 2 ​ j ) (\lambda^{2j-1},\lambda^{2j}) is an ( l − j ) (l-j) -falling pair. 




 




 
 Remark 3.2.

 
 In the remainder of this paper, we shall only be concerned with l l -oscillating sequences from λ \lambda to μ \mu where l ≥ ℓ ⁡ ( λ ) l\geq\ell(\lambda) . 





 
 For example, we take λ = ( 4 , 3 , 2 ) \lambda=(4,3,2) , μ = ( 4 , 3 , 3 , 1 ) \mu=(4,3,3,1) , and l = 3 l=3 . Figure 5 
gives an 3 3 -oscillating sequence from λ \lambda to μ \mu .

 Figure 5: An oscillating sequence from ( 4 , 3 , 2 ) (4,3,2) to ( 4 , 3 , 3 , 1 ) (4,3,3,1) with l = 3 l=3 . 
 
 
 Remark 3.3.

 
 By definition, it is easy to see that for an oscillating sequence S = ( λ 0 , λ 1 , … , λ 2 ​ l − 1 ) S=(\lambda^{0},\lambda^{1},\ldots,\lambda^{2l-1}) , the first parts of λ i \lambda^{i} ( 0 ≤ i ≤ 2 ​ l − 1 ) (0\leq i\leq 2l-1) are of the same size. 





 
 Let OS l ​ ( λ → μ ) \mathrm{OS}^{l}(\lambda\rightarrow\mu) denote the set of all l l -oscillating sequences from λ \lambda to μ \mu . Note that for fixed λ \lambda and μ \mu , the set OS l ​ ( λ → μ ) \mathrm{OS}^{l}(\lambda\rightarrow\mu) is finite since there are finitely many choices for the added vertical strips or the deleted horizontal strips in the construction process.
For an oscillating sequence S = ( λ 0 , … , λ 2 ​ l − 1 ) S=(\lambda^{0},\ldots,\lambda^{2l-1}) , define

 

 
 
 wt ⁡ ( S ) = ∏ 1 ≤ i ≤ l w l − i + 1 a i ​ ∏ 1 ≤ j ≤ l − 1 t l − j b j , a i = | λ 2 ​ i − 1 / λ 2 ​ i − 2 | , b j = | λ 2 ​ j − 1 / λ 2 ​ j | . \wt(S)=\prod_{1\leq i\leq l}w_{l-i+1}^{a_{i}}\prod_{1\leq j\leq l-1}t_{l-j}^{b_{j}},\hskip 20.00003pta_{i}=|\lambda^{2i-1}/\lambda^{2i-2}|,\hskip 10.00002ptb_{j}=|\lambda^{2j-1}/\lambda^{2j}|. 
 
 
 


 
 We relate set-valued reverse plane partitions to oscillating sequences in the following theorem.



 
 Theorem 3.4.

 
 For any fixed partition λ {\lambda} , there exists a bijection 

 

 
 
 Φ : SVRPP ⁡ ( λ ) ⟶ ⨆ μ SSYT ⁡ ( μ ) × OS ℓ ⁡ ( λ ) ⁡ ( λ → μ ) . \Phi:\SVRPP({\lambda})\longrightarrow\bigsqcup_{\mu}\SSYT({\mu})\times\OS^{\ell(\lambda)}({\lambda}\rightarrow{\mu}). 
 
 
 
 Moreover, for T ∈ SVRPP ⁡ ( λ ) T\in\SVRPP({\lambda}) ,
if Φ ⁡ ( T ) = ( T ′ , S ) \Phi(T)=(T^{\prime},S) , then 

 

 
 
 𝐱 ircont ⁡ ( T ) ​ 𝐰 ex ⁡ ( T ) ​ 𝐭 ceq ⁡ ( T ) = 𝐱 wt ⁡ ( T ′ ) ​ wt ⁡ ( S ) . \mathbf{x}^{\mathrm{ircont}(T)}\mathbf{w}^{\ex(T)}\mathbf{t}^{\ceq(T)}=\mathbf{x}^{\wt(T^{\prime})}{\wt(S)}. 
 
 
 




 
 As a direct consequence of Theorem 3.4 , we obtain a formula for the coefficients K λ , μ ​ ( 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) K_{{\lambda},{\mu}}(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}) in terms of oscillating sequences.



 
 Corollary 3.5.

 
 For partitions λ \lambda and μ \mu , we have 

 

 
 
 K λ , μ ​ ( 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) = ∑ α ∑ β f λ , μ ( α , β ) ​ 𝐰 α ​ 𝐭 β , K_{{\lambda},{\mu}}(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w})=\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{\beta}f_{{\lambda},{\mu}}^{(\alpha,\beta)}\mathbf{w}^{\alpha}\mathbf{t}^{\beta}, 
 
 
 
 where 

 

 
 
 f λ , μ ( α , β ) = # ⁡ { S ∈ OS ℓ ⁡ ( λ ) ⁡ ( λ → μ ) : wt ⁡ ( S ) = 𝐰 α ​ 𝐭 β } . f_{{\lambda},{\mu}}^{(\alpha,\beta)}=\#\{S\in\OS^{\ell({\lambda})}({\lambda}\rightarrow{\mu}):\ \wt(S)=\mathbf{w}^{\alpha}\mathbf{t}^{\beta}\}. 
 
 
 




 
 To prove Theorem 3.4 , we introduce an algorithm, called the dilation–contraction algorithm , performing on set-valued reverse plane partitions. The idea behind this is mainly inspired by the operations separately on set-valued tableaux and reverse plane partitions investigated by
Bandlow and Morse [ 2 ] .



 
 We first define the dilation operation.
For a vector v = ( v 1 , v 2 , … ) v=(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots) and r ≥ 1 r\geq 1 , we use v ≥ r v_{\geq r} to denote the truncation v ≥ r = ( v r , v r + 1 , … ) v_{\geq r}=(v_{r},v_{r+1},\ldots) starting from position r r .
For r , s ≥ 1 r,s\geq 1 , let SVRPP ⁡ ( λ , r , s ) \SVRPP(\lambda,r,s) denote the subset of SVRPP ⁡ ( λ ) \SVRPP(\lambda) including set-valued reverse plane partitions with ceq ≥ r ⁡ ( T ) = 𝟎 \ceq_{\geq r}(T)=\mathbf{0} and ex ≥ s ⁡ ( T ) = 𝟎 \ex_{\geq s}(T)=\mathbf{0} .
In other words, every box of T T in or below row r r has no redundant entry, and every box of T T in or below row s s is filled with a single number.



 
 For k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , the k k -th dilation operation, denoted di k \mathrm{di}_{k} , is performed on the set SVRPP ⁡ ( λ , k , k + 1 ) \SVRPP(\lambda,k,k+1) . Let T ∈ SVRPP ⁡ ( λ , k , k + 1 ) T\in\SVRPP(\lambda,k,k+1) . We use T ≥ k + 1 T_{\geq k+1} to denote the subtableau of T T occupying the boxes in and below row k + 1 k+1 .
Keep in mind that T ≥ k + 1 T_{\geq k+1} itself is an ordinary semistandard tableau. The image di k ​ ( T ) \mathrm{di}_{k}(T) of k k -th dilation acting on T T is defined as follows.

 
 • 
 
 Consider the entries in the k k -th row of T T by ignoring the smallest entry in each box. Suppose that such entries are x 1 > ⋯ > x m x_{1}>\cdots>x_{m} .
Remove the entries x 1 , … , x m x_{1},\ldots,x_{m} from largest to smallest, and meanwhile insert these entries successively into T ≥ k + 1 T_{\geq k+1} by applying the RSK algorithm.




 
 For example, in Figure 6 , we apply the dilation algorithm to a set-valued reverse partition of shape λ = ( 2 , 2 , 1 , 1 ) \lambda=(2,2,1,1) and with k = 2 k=2 . In the process, we insert ( x 1 , x 2 , x 3 ) = ( 4 , 3 , 2 ) (x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=(4,3,2) successively into T ≥ 3 T_{\geq 3} .

 Figure 6: An illustration of the dilation algorithm. 
 
 
 Lemma 3.6.

 
 Fix a shape λ \lambda . The map di k \mathrm{di}_{k} is a bijection from SVRPP ⁡ ( λ , k , k + 1 ) \SVRPP(\lambda,k,k+1) to the union of SVRPP ⁡ ( μ , k , k ) \SVRPP(\mu,k,k) , where ( λ , μ ) (\lambda,\mu) is a k k -rising pair. 





 
 Proof. 
Let T ∈ SVRPP ⁡ ( λ , k , k + 1 ) T\in\SVRPP(\lambda,k,k+1) , and T ′ = di k ​ ( T ) T^{\prime}=\mathrm{di}_{k}(T) . We first check that T ′ T^{\prime} belongs to SVRPP ⁡ ( μ , k , k ) \SVRPP(\mu,k,k) , where ( λ , μ ) (\lambda,\mu) is a k k -rising pair. Assume that x 1 > ⋯ > x m x_{1}>\cdots>x_{m} are the entries row k k , as used in the definition of the dilation. Let P 1 P_{1} be obtained from T T by removing x 1 x_{1} and then inserting x 1 x_{1} into T ≥ k + 1 T_{\geq k+1} . It is straightforward to check that P 1 P_{1} is a valid set-valued reverse plane partition with ceq ≥ k ⁡ ( P 1 ) = 𝟎 \ceq_{\geq k}(P_{1})=\mathbf{0} . Doing the same for x 2 , … , x m x_{2},\ldots,x_{m} yields the image T ′ T^{\prime} which is also a valid set-valued reverse plane partition with ceq ≥ k ⁡ ( T ′ ) = 𝟎 \ceq_{\geq k}(T^{\prime})=\mathbf{0} . It is also clear that ex ≥ k ⁡ ( T ′ ) = 𝟎 \ex_{\geq k}(T^{\prime})=\mathbf{0} .
In view of Proposition 2.1 , the difference of shapes of T ′ T^{\prime} and T T is a vertical strip. So we conclude that T ′ ∈ SVRPP ⁡ ( μ , k , k ) T^{\prime}\in\SVRPP(\mu,k,k) , where ( λ , μ ) (\lambda,\mu) is a k k -rising pair.



 
 Conversely, let T ′ T^{\prime} ∈ SVRPP ⁡ ( μ , k , k ) \in\SVRPP(\mu,k,k) , where ( λ , μ ) (\lambda,\mu) is a k k -rising pair. Do the reverse RSK to the entries in the vertical strip μ / λ \mu/\lambda from bottom to top, giving rise to a sequence x 1 > ⋯ > x m x_{1}>\cdots>x_{m} of bumped entries. Place these x 1 > ⋯ > x m x_{1}>\cdots>x_{m} into the boxes in row k k in a unique way such that row k k keeps weakly increasing. It is not hard to check that the final outcome is a set-valued reverse plane partition T T in SVRPP ⁡ ( λ , k , k + 1 ) \SVRPP(\lambda,k,k+1) .
Moreover, by the above construction, we have di k ​ ( T ) = T ′ \mathrm{di}_{k}(T)=T^{\prime} . This allows us to conclude the lemma.
 ■ \blacksquare 



 
 We next define the contraction operation. For k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , the k k -th contraction , denoted co k \mathrm{co}_{k} , acts on the set SVRPP ⁡ ( λ , k , k ) \SVRPP(\lambda,k,k) . Let T ∈ SVRPP ⁡ ( λ , k , k ) T\in\SVRPP(\lambda,k,k) . Equivalently, the subtableau T ≥ k T_{\geq k} is an ordinary semistandard tableau. The image co k ​ ( T ) \mathrm{co}_{k}(T) is constructed as follows.

 
 • 
 
 Replace each box in the k k -th row of T T , whose entry equals the largest entry of the set immediately above it in row k − 1 k-1 , by an empty box. Then apply the jdt slides to move these empty boxes down and to the right until they exit the diagram. The slide order is from right to left, thus every original empty box can be viewed as an inside corner.
This procedure can be best understood by an example as given in Figure 7 where k = 2 k=2 .

 Figure 7: An illustration of the contraction algorithm. 
 
 
 Lemma 3.7.

 
 Fix a shape λ \lambda . The map co k \mathrm{co}_{k} is a bijection from SVRPP ⁡ ( λ , k , k ) \SVRPP(\lambda,k,k) to the union of SVRPP ⁡ ( μ , k − 1 , k ) \SVRPP(\mu,k-1,k) , where ( λ , μ ) (\lambda,\mu) is a ( k − 1 ) (k-1) -falling pair. 





 
 Proof. 
Let T ∈ SVRPP ⁡ ( λ , k , k ) T\in\SVRPP({\lambda},k,k) . We can forget all the entries of T T in row k − 1 k-1 except the largest one in every box and add them back after the whole process.
Suppose that the empty boxes in row k k are ( k , j 1 ) , … , ( k , j m ) (k,j_{1}),\ldots,(k,j_{m}) , listed from right to left. By the construction of co k \co_{k} , we successively perform the jdt algorithm starting from the empty boxes in the order ( k , j 1 ) , … , ( k , j m ) (k,j_{1}),\ldots,(k,j_{m}) . A crucial observation is that the m m slide paths do not intersect, distributed from upper right to lower left.
Denote by T ′ T^{\prime} the resulting filling. Now it is a routine task to check that T ′ T^{\prime} is a set-valued reverse plane partition belonging to SVRPP ⁡ ( μ , k − 1 , k ) \SVRPP(\mu,k-1,k) , where λ / μ \lambda/\mu is a horizontal strip (so ( λ , μ ) (\lambda,\mu) is a ( k − 1 ) (k-1) -falling pair).



 
 Conversely, consider a T ′ ∈ SVRPP ⁡ ( μ , k − 1 , k ) T^{\prime}\in\SVRPP(\mu,k-1,k) , where ( λ , μ ) (\lambda,\mu) is a ( k − 1 ) (k-1) -falling pair. Then do the reverse jdt slides to these empty boxes in the horizontal strip λ / μ \lambda/\mu from left to right, and terminate at row k k (which means the next jdt slide will move the empty box up into row k − 1 k-1 ). Then fill every empty box in row k k with the largest entry in the box immediately above it. It is not hard to check that the final outcome is a set-valued reverse plane partition T T in SVRPP ⁡ ( λ , k , k ) \SVRPP(\lambda,k,k) . Moreover, the above construction is indeed the reverse map of co k \co_{k} . So the proof is complete.
 ■ \blacksquare 



 
 We can now describe our dilation-contraction algorithm
which transforms a set-valued reverse plane partition of shape λ \lambda to a semistandard tableau of shape μ \mu together with an ℓ ⁡ ( λ ) \ell(\lambda) -oscillating sequence from λ \lambda to μ \mu .
Let T ∈ SVRPP ⁡ ( λ ) T\in\SVRPP(\lambda) . We first use the dilation and contraction operations to generate a sequence ( T 0 , T 1 , … , T 2 ​ ℓ ​ ( λ ) − 1 ) (T_{0},T_{1},\ldots,T_{2\ell(\lambda)-1}) of set-valued reverse plane partitions. Set l = ℓ ⁡ ( λ ) l=\ell(\lambda) and T 0 = T T_{0}=T .
The tableaux T i T_{i} for i = 1 , … , 2 ​ l − 1 i=1,\ldots,2l-1 are constructed as follows.

 
 • 
 
 Let 1 ≤ i ≤ 2 ​ l − 1 1\leq i\leq 2l-1 . If i = 2 ​ k − 1 i=2k-1 is odd, then let

 

 
 
 T i = di l + 1 − k ​ ( T i − 1 ) T_{i}=\mathrm{di}_{l+1-k}(T_{i-1}) 
 
 
 
 be obtained from T i − 1 T_{i-1} by applying the ( l + 1 − k ) (l+1-k) -th dilation. If i = 2 ​ k i=2k is even, then let

 

 
 
 T i = co l + 1 − k ​ ( T i − 1 ) T_{i}=\mathrm{co}_{l+1-k}(T_{i-1}) 
 
 
 
 be obtained from T i − 1 T_{i-1} by applying the ( l + 1 − k ) (l+1-k) -th contraction.
That is,

 

 
 
 T 0 → di l T 1 → co l T 2 → di l − 1 T 3 → co l − 1 T 4 → di l − 2 ⋯ → co 2 T 2 ​ l − 2 → di 1 T 2 ​ l − 1 . T_{0}\xrightarrow{\text{$\mathrm{di}_{l}$}}T_{1}\xrightarrow{\text{$\mathrm{co}_{l}$}}T_{2}\xrightarrow{\text{$\mathrm{di}_{l-1}$}}T_{3}\xrightarrow{\text{$\mathrm{co}_{l-1}$}}T_{4}\xrightarrow{\text{$\mathrm{di}_{l-2}$}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\text{$\mathrm{co}_{2}$}}T_{2l-2}\xrightarrow{\text{$\mathrm{di}_{1}$}}T_{2l-1}. 
 
 
 



 
 Figure 8 gives a concrete example to illustrate the dilation-contraction algorithm.
