 Figure 8: An illustration of the dilation-contraction algorithm. 
 
 
 We can now construct the map Φ \Phi 
in Theorem 3.4 . For T ∈ SVRPP ⁡ ( λ ) T\in\SVRPP(\lambda) , let ( T 0 , T 1 , … , T 2 ​ ℓ ​ ( λ ) − 1 ) (T_{0},T_{1},\ldots,T_{2\ell(\lambda)-1}) be the sequence of set-valued reverse partitions after applying the dilation-contraction algorithm.
Notice that T 2 ​ ℓ ​ ( λ ) − 1 T_{2\ell(\lambda)-1} is an ordinary semistandard tableau. For 0 ≤ i ≤ 2 ​ ℓ ​ ( λ ) − 1 0\leq i\leq 2\ell(\lambda)-1 , write λ i \lambda^{i} for the shape of T i T_{i} . Define Φ ⁡ ( T ) = ( T ′ , S ) \Phi(T)=(T^{\prime},S) where T ′ = T 2 ​ ℓ ​ ( λ ) − 1 T^{\prime}=T_{2\ell(\lambda)-1} and

 

 
 
 S = ( λ 0 , λ 1 , … , λ 2 ​ ℓ ​ ( λ ) − 1 ) . S=(\lambda^{0},\lambda^{1},\ldots,\lambda^{2\ell(\lambda)-1}). 
 
 
 


 
 Proof of Theorem 3.4 . 
By Lemmas 3.6 and 3.7 , it is an easy task to justify that the map Φ \Phi is indeed a bijection from SVRPP ⁡ ( λ ) \SVRPP({\lambda}) to

 

 
 
 ⨆ μ SSYT ⁡ ( μ ) × OS ℓ ⁡ ( λ ) ⁡ ( λ → μ ) . \bigsqcup_{\mu}\SSYT({\mu})\times\OS^{\ell(\lambda)}({\lambda}\rightarrow{\mu}). 
 
 
 
 Moreover, from the definitions of ircont ⁡ ( T ) \mathrm{ircont}(T) , ex ⁡ ( T ) \ex(T) , ceq ⁡ ( T ) \ceq(T) and wt ⁡ ( S ) \wt(S) , we can directly check that

 

 
 
 𝐱 ircont ⁡ ( T ) ​ 𝐰 ex ⁡ ( T ) ​ 𝐭 ceq ⁡ ( T ) = 𝐱 wt ⁡ ( T ′ ) ​ wt ⁡ ( S ) . \mathbf{x}^{\mathrm{ircont}(T)}\mathbf{w}^{\ex(T)}\mathbf{t}^{\ceq(T)}=\mathbf{x}^{\wt(T^{\prime})}{\wt(S)}. 
 
 
 
 This completes the proof.
 ■ \blacksquare 



 
 Remark 3.8.

 
 For a partition λ \lambda , let λ ¯ = ( λ ¯ 1 , λ ¯ 2 , … ) \overline{{\lambda}}=(\overline{{\lambda}}_{1},\overline{{\lambda}}_{2},\ldots) be given by 

 

 
 
 λ ¯ i := min ⁡ { λ j + j − 1 : 1 ≤ j ≤ i } . \overline{{\lambda}}_{i}:=\min\{{\lambda}_{j}+j-1:1\leq j\leq i\}. 
 
 
 
 By [ 13 , Section 4] , 

 

 
 
 ( λ 1 ) ⊆ μ ⊆ λ ¯ ⟺ K λ , μ ​ ( 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) ≠ 0 . ({\lambda}_{1})\subseteq{\mu}\subseteq\overline{{\lambda}}\Longleftrightarrow K_{{\lambda},{\mu}}(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w})\neq 0. 
 
 
 
 (In [ 13 , Section 4] , we defined λ ¯ ( n ) \overline{{\lambda}}^{(n)} and here we need to let n → ∞ n\rightarrow\infty to obtain λ ¯ \overline{{\lambda}} .)
It should be noted that λ ¯ \overline{{\lambda}} is regarded as a weakly decreasing sequence since it has infinitely many nonzero parts, and the notation μ ⊆ λ ¯ {\mu}\subseteq\overline{{\lambda}} means that μ i ≤ λ ¯ i \mu_{i}\leq\overline{{\lambda}}_{i} for i ≥ 1 i\geq 1 .
Combining Corollary 3.5 ,
we have 

 

 
 
 ( λ 1 ) ⊆ μ ⊆ λ ¯ ⟺ OS ℓ ⁡ ( λ ) ⁡ ( λ → μ ) ≠ ∅ . ({\lambda}_{1})\subseteq{\mu}\subseteq\overline{{\lambda}}\Longleftrightarrow\OS^{\ell(\lambda)}({\lambda}\rightarrow{\mu})\neq\emptyset. 
 
 
 






 
 3.2 Distributive lattice of oscillating sequences


 
 We show that oscillating sequences admit a natural distributive lattice structure. A crucial observation is as follows.



 
 Lemma 3.9.

 
 If ( λ , ρ ) (\lambda,\rho) and ( μ , γ ) (\mu,\gamma) are k k -rising pairs (resp., k k -falling pairs), then ( λ ∨ μ , ρ ∨ γ ) (\lambda\vee\mu,\rho\vee\gamma) and
 ( λ ∧ μ , ρ ∧ γ ) (\lambda\wedge\mu,\rho\wedge\gamma) 
are also k k -rising pairs (resp., k k -falling pairs). 





 
 Proof. 
We only verify the case for k k -rising pairs. The proof for k k -falling pairs can be dealt with in an analogous manner.



 
 For
 1 ≤ i ≤ k 1\leq i\leq k , it follows from the definition that λ i = ρ i \lambda_{i}=\rho_{i} and μ i = γ i \mu_{i}=\gamma_{i} , and so we have

 

 
 
 ( λ ∨ μ ) i = max ⁡ { λ i , μ i } = max ⁡ { ρ i , γ i } = ( ρ ∨ γ ) i , (\lambda\vee\mu)_{i}=\max\{\lambda_{i},\mu_{i}\}=\max\{\rho_{i},\gamma_{i}\}=(\rho\vee\gamma)_{i}, 
 
 
 
 and

 

 
 
 ( λ ∧ μ ) i = min ⁡ { λ i , μ i } = min ⁡ { ρ i , γ i } = ( ρ ∧ γ ) i . (\lambda\wedge\mu)_{i}=\min\{\lambda_{i},\mu_{i}\}=\min\{\rho_{i},\gamma_{i}\}=(\rho\wedge\gamma)_{i}. 
 
 
 
 It remains to show that both ( ρ ∨ γ ) / ( λ ∨ μ ) (\rho\vee\gamma)/(\lambda\vee\mu) and ( ρ ∧ γ ) / ( λ ∧ μ ) (\rho\wedge\gamma)/(\lambda\wedge\mu) 
are vertical strips.



 
 Notice that if λ ⊆ ρ \lambda\subseteq\rho and
 μ ⊆ γ \mu\subseteq\gamma , then

 

 
 
 λ ∨ μ ⊆ ρ ∨ γ and λ ∧ μ ⊆ ρ ∧ γ . \lambda\vee\mu\subseteq\rho\vee\gamma\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\lambda\wedge\mu\subseteq\rho\wedge\gamma. 
 
 
 
 Since both ρ / λ \rho/\lambda and γ / μ \gamma/\mu are vertical strips, we see that for j ≥ 1 j\geq 1 ,

 

 
 
 ρ j − λ j ≤ 1 , γ j − μ j ≤ 1 . \rho_{j}-\lambda_{j}\leq 1,\hskip 20.00003pt\gamma_{j}-\mu_{j}\leq 1. 
 
 
 
 Thus, for j ≥ 1 j\geq 1 , we deduce that

 

 
 
 ( ρ ∨ γ ) j − ( λ ∨ μ ) j \displaystyle(\rho\vee\gamma)_{j}-(\lambda\vee\mu)_{j} 
 = max ⁡ { ρ j , γ j } − max ⁡ { λ j , μ j } \displaystyle=\max\{\rho_{j},\gamma_{j}\}-\max\{\lambda_{j},\mu_{j}\} 
 
 
 
 
 
 = max ⁡ { ρ j − max ⁡ { λ j , μ j } , γ j − max ⁡ { λ j , μ j } } \displaystyle=\max\{\rho_{j}-\max\{\lambda_{j},\mu_{j}\},\,\gamma_{j}-\max\{\lambda_{j},\mu_{j}\}\} 
 
 
 
 
 
 ≤ max ⁡ { ρ j − λ j , γ j − μ j } ≤ 1 , \displaystyle\leq\max\{\rho_{j}-\lambda_{j},\gamma_{j}-\mu_{j}\}\leq 1, 
 
 
 
 and

 

 
 
 ( ρ ∧ γ ) j − ( λ ∧ μ ) j \displaystyle(\rho\wedge\gamma)_{j}-(\lambda\wedge\mu)_{j} 
 = min ⁡ { ρ j , γ j } − min ⁡ { λ j , μ j } \displaystyle=\min\{\rho_{j},\gamma_{j}\}-\min\{\lambda_{j},\mu_{j}\} 
 
 
 
 
 
 = min ⁡ { ρ j , γ j } + max ⁡ { − λ j , − μ j } \displaystyle=\min\{\rho_{j},\gamma_{j}\}+\max\{-\lambda_{j},-\mu_{j}\} 
 
 
 
 
 
 = max ⁡ { min ⁡ { ρ j , γ j } − λ j , min ⁡ { ρ j , γ j } − μ j } \displaystyle=\max\{\min\{\rho_{j},\gamma_{j}\}-\lambda_{j},\,\min\{\rho_{j},\gamma_{j}\}-\mu_{j}\} 
 
 
 
 
 
 ≤ max ⁡ { ρ j − λ j , γ j − μ j } ≤ 1 , \displaystyle\leq\max\{\rho_{j}-\lambda_{j},\gamma_{j}-\mu_{j}\}\leq 1, 
 
 
 
 which implies that ( ρ ∨ γ ) / ( λ ∨ μ ) (\rho\vee\gamma)/(\lambda\vee\mu) and ( ρ ∧ γ ) / ( λ ∧ μ ) (\rho\wedge\gamma)/(\lambda\wedge\mu) 
are vertical strips.
 ■ \blacksquare 



 
 Denote

 

 
 
 OS l ( λ ) = ⋃ μ OS l ( λ → μ ) = ⋃ μ : ( λ 1 ) ⊆ μ ⊆ λ ¯ OS l ( λ → μ ) , \OS^{l}({\lambda})=\bigcup_{\mu}\OS^{l}(\lambda\to\mu)=\bigcup_{\mu:({\lambda}_{1})\subseteq{\mu}\subseteq\overline{{\lambda}}}\OS^{l}(\lambda\to\mu), 
 
 
 
 where the second equality follows from
Remark 3.8 .
We also use the notation

 

 
 
 OS k l = ⋃ λ : ℓ ⁡ ( λ ) ≤ l , λ 1 ≤ k OS l ( λ ) , \mathrm{OS}^{l}_{k}=\bigcup_{\lambda:\,\ell(\lambda)\leq l,\,\lambda_{1}\leq k}\mathrm{OS}^{l}(\lambda), 
 
 
 
 and

 

 
 
 OS k l , m = ⋃ λ : ℓ ⁡ ( λ ) ≤ l λ 1 ≤ k ⋃ μ : ℓ ⁡ ( μ ) ≤ m ( λ 1 ) ⊆ μ ⊆ λ ¯ OS l ( λ → μ ) . \OS^{l,m}_{k}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\lambda}:\ell({\lambda})\leq l\\
{\lambda}_{1}\leq k\end{subarray}}\ \bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu:\ell({\mu})\leq m\\
({\lambda}_{1})\subseteq{\mu}\subseteq\overline{{\lambda}}\end{subarray}}\OS^{l}({\lambda}\rightarrow{\mu}). 
 
 (3.1) 
 
 


 
 The following is a consequence of Lemma 3.9 .



 
 Corollary 3.10.

 
 For two l l -oscillating sequences S = ( λ 0 , λ 1 , … , λ 2 ​ l − 1 ) S=(\lambda^{0},\lambda^{1},\ldots,\lambda^{2l-1}) and S ′ = ( μ 0 , μ 1 , … , μ 2 ​ l − 1 ) S^{\prime}=(\mu^{0},\mu^{1},\ldots,\mu^{2l-1}) ,
define 

 

 
 
 S ∨ S ′ = ( λ 0 ∨ μ 0 , λ 1 ∨ μ 1 , … , λ 2 ​ l − 1 ∨ μ 2 ​ l − 1 ) S\vee S^{\prime}=(\lambda^{0}\vee\mu^{0},\lambda^{1}\vee\mu^{1},\ldots,\lambda^{2l-1}\vee\mu^{2l-1}) 
 
 
 
 and 

 

 
 
 S ∧ S ′ = ( λ 0 ∧ μ 0 , λ 1 ∧ μ 1 , … , λ 2 ​ l − 1 ∧ μ 2 ​ l − 1 ) . S\wedge S^{\prime}=(\lambda^{0}\wedge\mu^{0},\lambda^{1}\wedge\mu^{1},\ldots,\lambda^{2l-1}\wedge\mu^{2l-1}). 
 
 
 
 Then S ∨ S ′ S\vee S^{\prime} and S ∧ S ′ S\wedge S^{\prime} are also l l -oscillating sequences. With the above join and meet, the sets OS l ⁡ ( λ → μ ) \OS^{l}(\lambda\to\mu) , OS l ⁡ ( λ ) \OS^{l}(\lambda) ,
 OS k l \OS^{l}_{k} and OS k l , m \OS^{l,m}_{k} are all distributive lattices. In particular, the first and the last are finite distributive lattices. 









 
 4 Multivariate Reuter–Lovász–Saks inequality


 
 In this section, we present a multivariate Reuter–Lovász–Saks type inequality which is another ingredient in the proof of Theorem 1.5 .
For the history on the classical Reuter–Lovász–Saks inequality, we refer the reader to [ 4 ] for an overview.



 
 Throughout this section, let ( L , ∧ , ∨ ) ({L},\wedge,\vee) be a finite distributive lattice.
Fix variables 𝐪 = ( q 1 , … , q N ) \mathbf{q}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{N}) , and modular functions 
 𝐫 = ( r 1 , … , r N ) \mathbf{r}=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{N}) , that is, for 1 ≤ i ≤ N 1\leq i\leq N , r i : L → ℤ ≥ 0 r_{i}:{L}\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} satisfies

 

 
 
 r i ​ ( x ) + r i ​ ( y ) = r i ​ ( x ∨ y ) + r i ​ ( x ∧ y ) for  ​ x , y ∈ L . r_{i}(x)+r_{i}(y)=r_{i}(x\vee y)+r_{i}(x\wedge y)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for }x,y\in{L}. 
 
 (4.1) 
 
 
 For x ∈ L x\in{L} , write
 𝐪 𝐫 ⁡ ( x ) := q 1 r 1 ​ ( x ) ⋯ q N r N ​ ( x ) \mathbf{q}^{\mathbf{r}(x)}:=q_{1}^{r_{1}(x)}\cdots q_{N}^{r_{N}(x)} .
For a function ρ : L → ℝ ≥ 0 \rho:{L}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0} and subset X ⊆ L X\subseteq{L} , define

 

 
 
 ρ ⟨ 𝐪 , 𝐫 ⟩ ​ ( X ) := ∑ x ∈ X ρ ⁡ ( x ) ​ 𝐪 𝐫 ⁡ ( x ) ∈ ℝ ≥ 0 ​ [ q 1 , … , q N ] . \rho_{\langle\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r}\rangle}(X):=\sum_{x\in X}\rho(x)\mathbf{q}^{\mathbf{r}(x)}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}[q_{1},\ldots,q_{N}]. 
 
 
 
 For two polynomials f , g f,g in 𝐪 \mathbf{q} , write f ≤ 𝐪 g f\leq^{\mathbf{q}}g 
if

 

 
 
 g − f ∈ ℝ ≥ 0 ​ [ 𝐪 ] . g-f\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}[\mathbf{q}]. 
 
 
 


 
 For two subset X , Y ⊆ L X,Y\subseteq L , let X ∨ Y = { x ∨ y : x ∈ X , y ∈ Y } X\vee Y=\{x\vee y\colon x\in X,y\in Y\} and
 X ∧ Y = { x ∧ y : x ∈ X , y ∈ Y } X\wedge Y=\{x\wedge y\colon x\in X,y\in Y\} . The following is a multivariate Reuter–Lovász–Saks type inequality.



 
 Theorem 4.1 (multivariate RLS type inequality) .

 
 Let ( L , ∧ , ∨ ) (L,\wedge,\vee) be a finite distributive lattice, and let
 r 1 , … , r N : L → ℤ ≥ 0 r_{1},\ldots,r_{N}:L\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} 
be modular functions satisfying ( 4.1 ).
Suppose that for every subset
 H ⊆ [ n ] := { 1 , … , n } H\subseteq[n]:=\{1,\ldots,n\} , we are given subsets 

 

 
 
 A H , B H , C H , D H ⊆ L A_{H},\ B_{H},\ C_{H},\ D_{H}\subseteq L 
 
 
 
 such that for any I , J ⊆ [ n ] I,J\subseteq[n] ,
 A I ∨ B J ⊆ C I ∪ J A_{I}\vee B_{J}\subseteq C_{I\cup J} and A I ∧ B J ⊆ D I ∩ J . A_{I}\wedge B_{J}\subseteq D_{I\cap J}. 
For a subset X ⊆ L X\subseteq L , define 

 

 
 
 X ⁡ ( 𝐪 ) = ∑ x ∈ X 𝐪 𝐫 ⁡ ( x ) . X(\mathbf{q})=\sum_{x\in X}\mathbf{q}^{\mathbf{r}(x)}. 
 
 
 
 Then 

 

 
 
 ∑ H ⊆ [ n ] A H ​ ( 𝐪 ) ​ B H ¯ ​ ( 𝐪 ) ≤ ∑ H ⊆ [ n ] 𝐪 ⁡ C H ​ ( 𝐪 ) ​ D H ¯ ​ ( 𝐪 ) , where  H ¯ = [ n ] ∖ H . \sum_{H\subseteq[n]}A_{H}(\mathbf{q})B_{\overline{H}}(\mathbf{q})\leq^{\mathbf{q}}\sum_{H\subseteq[n]}C_{H}(\mathbf{q})D_{\overline{H}}(\mathbf{q}),\ \ \ \text{where $\overline{H}=[n]\setminus H$}. 
 
 
 




 
 To give a proof of Theorem 4.1 , we need the multivariate Ahlswede–Daykin inequality due to Chan and Pak [ 5 , Theorem 6.1] .



 
 Theorem 4.2 ( [ 5 , Theorem 6.1] ) .

 
 Let ( L , ∧ , ∨ ) ({L},\wedge,\vee) be a finite distributive lattice, and let
 α , β , ρ , δ : L → ℝ ≥ 0 \alpha,\beta,\rho,\delta:{L}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0} be nonnegative functions on L {L} .
Suppose that 

 

 
 
 α ⁡ ( x ) ⋅ β ⁡ ( y ) ≤ ρ ⁡ ( x ∨ y ) ⋅ δ ⁡ ( x ∧ y ) for  ​ x , y ∈ L . \alpha(x)\cdot\beta(y)\leq\rho(x\vee y)\cdot\delta(x\wedge y)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for }x,y\in{L}. 
 
 
 
 Then, for X , Y ⊆ L X,Y\subseteq{L} , we have 

 

 
 
 α ⟨ 𝐪 , 𝐫 ⟩ ( X ) ⋅ β ⟨ 𝐪 , 𝐫 ⟩ ( Y ) ≤ 𝐪 ρ ⟨ 𝐪 , 𝐫 ⟩ ( X ∨ Y ) ⋅ δ ⟨ 𝐪 , 𝐫 ⟩ ( X ∧ Y ) . \alpha_{\langle\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r}\rangle}(X)\cdot\beta_{\langle\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r}\rangle}(Y)\leq^{\mathbf{q}}\rho_{\langle\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r}\rangle}(X\vee Y)\cdot\delta_{\langle\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r}\rangle}(X\wedge Y). 
 
 
 




 
 We now give a proof of Theorem 4.1 .



 
 Proof of Theorem 4.1 
We first encode a subset H ⊆ [ n ] H\subseteq[n] by extra variables
 𝐳 = ( z 1 , … , z n ) \mathbf{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}) , that is, write

 

 
 
 𝐳 H = ∏ i ∈ H z i . \mathbf{z}^{H}=\prod_{i\in H}z_{i}. 
 
 
 
 Consider the product lattice

 

 
 
 L ~ = L × 2 [ n ] , \widetilde{L}=L\times 2^{[n]}, 
 
 
 
 with join and meet given by

 

 
 
 ( x , H ) ∨ ( y , J ) = ( x ∨ y , H ∪ J ) , ( x , H ) ∧ ( y , J ) = ( x ∧ y , H ∩ J ) . (x,H)\vee(y,J)=(x\vee y,H\cup J),\ \ \ (x,H)\wedge(y,J)=(x\wedge y,H\cap J). 
 
 
 
 With the above operations,
 L ~ \widetilde{L} is a finite distributive lattice.



 
 We extend the modular functions on L L to L ~ \widetilde{L} by setting

 

 
 
 r ~ i ​ ( x , H ) = r i ​ ( x ) , 1 ≤ i ≤ N . \widetilde{r}_{i}(x,H)=r_{i}(x),\hskip 20.00003pt1\leq i\leq N. 
 
 
 
 Moreover, we introduce Boolean modular functions: for 1 ≤ j ≤ n 1\leq j\leq n ,

 

 
 
 ε j ​ ( x , H ) = { 1 , j ∈ H , 0 , j ∉ H . \varepsilon_{j}(x,H)=\begin{cases}1,&j\in H,\\
0,&j\notin H.\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 Indeed, one has

 

 
 
 ε j ​ ( x , H ) + ε j ​ ( y , J ) = ε j ​ ( x ∨ y , H ∪ J ) + ε j ​ ( x ∧ y , H ∩ J ) , \varepsilon_{j}(x,H)+\varepsilon_{j}(y,J)=\varepsilon_{j}(x\vee y,H\cup J)+\varepsilon_{j}(x\wedge y,H\cap J), 
 
 
 
 because for each fixed j ∈ [ n ] j\in[n] ,

 

 
 
 𝟏 j ∈ H + 𝟏 j ∈ J = 𝟏 j ∈ H ∪ J + 𝟏 j ∈ H ∩ J . \mathbf{1}_{j\in H}+\mathbf{1}_{j\in J}=\mathbf{1}_{j\in H\cup J}+\mathbf{1}_{j\in H\cap J}. 
 
 
 
 Now we see that r ~ 1 , … , r ~ N , ε 1 , … , ε n \widetilde{r}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{r}_{N},\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n} 
are modular functions on L ~ \widetilde{L} .



 
 Define four subsets of L ~ \widetilde{L} by

 

 
 
 𝒜 \displaystyle\mathcal{A} 
 = { ( a , H ) : H ⊆ [ n ] , a ∈ A H } , \displaystyle=\{(a,H):H\subseteq[n],\ a\in A_{H}\}, 
 
 
 
 
 ℬ \displaystyle\mathcal{B} 
 = { ( b , J ) : J ⊆ [ n ] , b ∈ B J } , \displaystyle=\{(b,J):J\subseteq[n],\ b\in B_{J}\}, 
 
 
 
 
 𝒞 \displaystyle\mathcal{C} 
 = { ( c , K ) : K ⊆ [ n ] , c ∈ C K } , \displaystyle=\{(c,K):K\subseteq[n],\ c\in C_{K}\}, 
 
 
 
 
 𝒟 \displaystyle\mathcal{D} 
 = { ( d , M ) : M ⊆ [ n ] , d ∈ D M } . \displaystyle=\{(d,M):M\subseteq[n],\ d\in D_{M}\}. 
 
 
 
 We claim that

 

 
 
 𝒜 ∨ ℬ ⊆ 𝒞 , 𝒜 ∧ ℬ ⊆ 𝒟 . \mathcal{A}\vee\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{C},\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{A}\wedge\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{D}. 
 
 
 
 This can be seen as follows. If ( a , H ) ∈ 𝒜 (a,H)\in\mathcal{A} and ( b , J ) ∈ ℬ (b,J)\in\mathcal{B} , then
 a ∈ A H a\in A_{H} and b ∈ B J b\in B_{J} . By the assumption in the theorem, we have

 

 
 
 a ∨ b ∈ C H ∪ J , a ∧ b ∈ D H ∩ J . a\vee b\in C_{H\cup J},\hskip 20.00003pta\wedge b\in D_{H\cap J}. 
 
 
 
 Therefore

 

 
 
 ( a , H ) ∨ ( b , J ) = ( a ∨ b , H ∪ J ) ∈ 𝒞 , (a,H)\vee(b,J)=(a\vee b,H\cup J)\in\mathcal{C}, 
 
 
 
 and

 

 
 
 ( a , H ) ∧ ( b , J ) = ( a ∧ b , H ∩ J ) ∈ 𝒟 . (a,H)\wedge(b,J)=(a\wedge b,H\cap J)\in\mathcal{D}. 
 
 
 
 This verifies the claim.



 
 Applying Theorem 4.2 , along with the above claim, to L ~ \widetilde{L} with
 α = β = ρ = δ = 1 \alpha=\beta=\rho=\delta=1 
and with the modular functions
 r ~ 1 , … , r ~ N , ε 1 , … , ε n \widetilde{r}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{r}_{N},\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n} , we deduce that

 

 
 
 ( ∑ ( a , H ) ∈ 𝒜 𝐪 𝐫 ⁡ ( a ) ​ 𝐳 H ) ​ ( ∑ ( b , J ) ∈ ℬ 𝐪 𝐫 ⁡ ( b ) ​ 𝐳 J ) \displaystyle\left(\sum_{(a,H)\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{q}^{{\mathbf{r}}(a)}\mathbf{z}^{H}\right)\left(\sum_{(b,J)\in\mathcal{B}}\mathbf{q}^{{\mathbf{r}}(b)}\mathbf{z}^{J}\right) 
 ≤ 𝐪 , 𝐳 ( ∑ ( c , K ) ∈ 𝒜 ∨ ℬ 𝐪 𝐫 ⁡ ( c ) 𝐳 K ) ( ∑ ( d , M ) ∈ A ∧ ℬ 𝐪 𝐫 ⁡ ( d ) 𝐳 M ) \displaystyle\leq^{\mathbf{q},\mathbf{z}}\left(\sum_{(c,K)\in\mathcal{A}\vee\mathcal{B}}\mathbf{q}^{{\mathbf{r}}(c)}\mathbf{z}^{K}\right)\left(\sum_{(d,M)\in\mathcal{\mathcal{}}A\wedge\mathcal{B}}\mathbf{q}^{{\mathbf{r}}(d)}\mathbf{z}^{M}\right) 
 
 
 
 
 
 ≤ 𝐪 , 𝐳 ( ∑ ( c , K ) ∈ 𝒞 𝐪 𝐫 ⁡ ( c ) 𝐳 K ) ( ∑ ( d , M ) ∈ 𝒟 𝐪 𝐫 ⁡ ( d ) 𝐳 M ) . \displaystyle\leq^{\mathbf{q},\mathbf{z}}\left(\sum_{(c,K)\in\mathcal{C}}\mathbf{q}^{{\mathbf{r}}(c)}\mathbf{z}^{K}\right)\left(\sum_{(d,M)\in\mathcal{D}}\mathbf{q}^{{\mathbf{r}}(d)}\mathbf{z}^{M}\right). 
 
 
 
 Equivalently,

 

 
 
 ( ∑ H ⊆ [ n ] A H ( 𝐪 ) 𝐳 H ) ( ∑ J ⊆ [ n ] B J ( 𝐪 ) 𝐳 J ) ≤ 𝐪 , 𝐳 ( ∑ K ⊆ [ n ] C K ( 𝐪 ) 𝐳 K ) ( ∑ M ⊆ [ n ] D M ( 𝐪 ) 𝐳 M ) . \left(\sum_{H\subseteq[n]}A_{H}(\mathbf{q})\mathbf{z}^{H}\right)\left(\sum_{J\subseteq[n]}B_{J}(\mathbf{q})\mathbf{z}^{J}\right)\leq^{\mathbf{q},\mathbf{z}}\left(\sum_{K\subseteq[n]}C_{K}(\mathbf{q})\mathbf{z}^{K}\right)\left(\sum_{M\subseteq[n]}D_{M}(\mathbf{q})\mathbf{z}^{M}\right). 
 
 
 
 We now take the coefficients of
 𝐳 [ n ] = z 1 z 2 ⋯ z n \mathbf{z}^{[n]}=z_{1}z_{2}\cdots z_{n} on both sides.
Since each 𝐳 H \mathbf{z}^{H} is squarefree, the coefficient of
 𝐳 [ n ] \mathbf{z}^{[n]} on the left-hand side is obtained precisely from pairs
 ( H , J ) (H,J) satisfying
 H ∩ J = ∅ , H ∪ J = [ n ] H\cap J=\emptyset,H\cup J=[n] .
Equivalently, J = H ¯ . J=\overline{H}. 
Hence

 

 
 
 [ 𝐳 [ n ] ] ​ ( ∑ H ⊆ [ n ] A H ​ ( 𝐪 ) ​ 𝐳 H ) ​ ( ∑ J ⊆ [ n ] B J ​ ( 𝐪 ) ​ 𝐳 J ) = ∑ H ⊆ [ n ] A H ​ ( 𝐪 ) ​ B H ¯ ​ ( 𝐪 ) . \left[\mathbf{z}^{[n]}\right]\left(\sum_{H\subseteq[n]}A_{H}(\mathbf{q})\mathbf{z}^{H}\right)\left(\sum_{J\subseteq[n]}B_{J}(\mathbf{q})\mathbf{z}^{J}\right)=\sum_{H\subseteq[n]}A_{H}(\mathbf{q})B_{\overline{H}}(\mathbf{q}). 
 
 
 
 Similarly,

 

 
 
 [ 𝐳 [ n ] ] ​ ( ∑ K ⊆ [ n ] C K ​ ( 𝐪 ) ​ 𝐳 K ) ​ ( ∑ M ⊆ [ n ] D M ​ ( 𝐪 ) ​ 𝐳 M ) = ∑ K ⊆ [ n ] C K ​ ( 𝐪 ) ​ D K ¯ ​ ( 𝐪 ) . \left[\mathbf{z}^{[n]}\right]\left(\sum_{K\subseteq[n]}C_{K}(\mathbf{q})\mathbf{z}^{K}\right)\left(\sum_{M\subseteq[n]}D_{M}(\mathbf{q})\mathbf{z}^{M}\right)=\sum_{K\subseteq[n]}C_{K}(\mathbf{q})D_{\overline{K}}(\mathbf{q}). 
 
 
 
 Note that the inequality before taking coefficients is coefficientwise in
both 𝐪 \mathbf{q} and 𝐳 \mathbf{z} . Hence taking the coefficient of
 𝐳 [ n ] \mathbf{z}^{[n]} preserves coefficientwise nonnegativity in
 𝐪 \mathbf{q} . This implies that

 

 
 
 ∑ H ⊆ [ n ] A H ​ ( 𝐪 ) ​ B H ¯ ​ ( 𝐪 ) ≤ ∑ H ⊆ [ n ] 𝐪 ⁡ C H ​ ( 𝐪 ) ​ D H ¯ ​ ( 𝐪 ) , \sum_{H\subseteq[n]}A_{H}(\mathbf{q})B_{\overline{H}}(\mathbf{q})\leq^{\mathbf{q}}\sum_{H\subseteq[n]}C_{H}(\mathbf{q})D_{\overline{H}}(\mathbf{q}), 
 
 
 
 and so the proof is complete.
 ■ \blacksquare 





 
 5 Schur orchestra inequality


 
 In this section, we recall the Schur orchestra inequality developed by Chan, Chen, Pak and Soskin [ 4 ] . We then present a variation of this inequality which will be needed in the proof of Theorem 1.5 .



 
 Let

 

 
 
 ℰ = { 1 , … , ℓ } × { 0 } , ℱ = { 1 , … , ℓ } × { 1 } \mathcal{E}=\{1,\ldots,\ell\}\times\{0\},\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{F}=\{1,\ldots,\ell\}\times\{1\} 
 
 
 
 be two disjoint copies of [ ℓ ] [\ell] . Denote by ℰ ⊔ ℱ \mathcal{E}\sqcup\mathcal{F} 
their disjoint union.
Let

 

 
 
 O : 2 ℰ ⊔ ℱ ⟶ ℝ O:2^{\mathcal{E}\sqcup\mathcal{F}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R} 
 
 
 
 be a real-valued function on the Boolean lattice of subsets of
 ℰ ⊔ ℱ \mathcal{E}\sqcup\mathcal{F} .
We say that O O satisfies the orchestra inequalities if for every set partition ℰ ⊔ ℱ = B 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ B t \mathcal{E}\sqcup\mathcal{F}=B_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup B_{t} ,

 

 
 
 ∑ H ⊆ [ t ] O ⁡ ( ⨆ h ∈ H B h ) ≥ 0 . \sum_{H\subseteq[t]}O\!\left(\bigsqcup_{h\in H}B_{h}\right)\geq 0. 
 
 
 


 
 Suppose that

 

 
 
 α , β , γ , δ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ℓ . \alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{\ell}. 
 
 
 
 For a subset E ⊆ ℰ E\subseteq\mathcal{E} , define
 λ ⁡ ( E ) = ( λ 1 ​ ( E ) , … , λ ℓ ​ ( E ) ) \lambda(E)=(\lambda_{1}(E),\ldots,\lambda_{\ell}(E)) 
by

 

 
 
 λ i ​ ( E ) = { α i + γ i , i ∈ E , α i , i ∉ E , \lambda_{i}(E)=\begin{cases}\alpha_{i}+\gamma_{i},&i\in E,\\
\alpha_{i},&i\notin E,\end{cases} 
 
 (5.1) 
 
 
 where we simply use i ∈ E i\in E to represent ( i , 0 ) ∈ E (i,0)\in E .
Similarly, for a subset F ⊆ ℱ F\subseteq\mathcal{F} , define
 μ ⁡ ( F ) = ( μ 1 ​ ( F ) , … , μ ℓ ​ ( F ) ) \mu(F)=(\mu_{1}(F),\ldots,\mu_{\ell}(F)) 
by

 

 
 
 μ j ​ ( F ) = { β j + δ j , j ∈ F , β j , j ∉ F . \mu_{j}(F)=\begin{cases}\beta_{j}+\delta_{j},&j\in F,\\
\beta_{j},&j\notin F.\end{cases} 
 
 
 


 
 For E ⊆ ℰ E\subseteq\mathcal{E} and F ⊆ ℱ F\subseteq\mathcal{F} , write
 E ¯ = ℰ ∖ E \overline{E}=\mathcal{E}\setminus E and
 F ¯ = ℱ ∖ F \overline{F}=\mathcal{F}\setminus F . We also adopt the following convention: if λ \lambda or μ \mu is not a partition,
or if μ ⊈ λ \mu\not\subseteq\lambda , then
 s λ / μ = 0 s_{\lambda/\mu}=0 .



 
 Theorem 5.1 ( Schur orchestra inequality [ 4 , Theorem 5.2] ) .

 
 Let 

 

 
 
 O : 2 ℰ ⊔ ℱ ⟶ ℝ O:2^{\mathcal{E}\sqcup\mathcal{F}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R} 
 
 
 
 be a function satisfying the orchestra inequalities. Then 

 

 
 
 ∑ E ⊆ ℰ F ⊆ ℱ O ( E ⊔ F ) s λ ⁡ ( E ) / μ ⁡ ( F ) ( 𝐱 ) s λ ⁡ ( E ¯ ) / μ ⁡ ( F ¯ ) ( 𝐱 ) ≥ s 0 . \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}E\subseteq\mathcal{E}\\
F\subseteq\mathcal{F}\end{subarray}}O(E\sqcup F)\,s_{\lambda(E)/\mu(F)}(\mathbf{x})\,s_{\lambda(\overline{E})/\mu(\overline{F})}(\mathbf{x})\geq_{s}0. 
 
 
 




 
 What we require is a variation of the Schur orchestra inequality.



 
 Theorem 5.2.

 
 Let I = [ ℓ ] I=[\ell] , and let
 O : 2 I ⟶ ℝ O:2^{I}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R} 
be a function satisfying the orchestra inequalities: for every set partition
 I = B 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ B t I=B_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup B_{t} , 

 

 
 
 ∑ H ⊆ [ t ] O ⁡ ( ⋃ h ∈ H B h ) ≥ 0 . \sum_{H\subseteq[t]}O\left(\bigcup_{h\in H}B_{h}\right)\geq 0. 
 
 
 
 Let α , γ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ℓ \alpha,\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{\ell} . For E ⊆ I E\subseteq I , define λ ⁡ ( E ) \lambda(E) as in ( 5.1 ). Then 

 

 
 
 ∑ E ⊆ I O ( E ) s λ ⁡ ( E ) ( 𝐱 ) s λ ⁡ ( I ∖ E ) ( 𝐱 ) ≥ s 0 . \sum_{E\subseteq I}O(E)\,s_{\lambda(E)}(\mathbf{x})\,s_{\lambda(I\setminus E)}(\mathbf{x})\geq_{s}0. 
 
 
 




 
 Proof. 
Let

 

 
 
 ℰ = I × { 0 } , ℱ = I × { 1 } \mathcal{E}=I\times\{0\},\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{F}=I\times\{1\} 
 
 
 
 be two disjoint copies of I I . For A ⊆ ℰ A\subseteq\mathcal{E} , define the projection

 

 
 
 π ⁡ ( A ) = { i ∈ I : ( i , 0 ) ∈ A } . \pi(A)=\{i\in I:(i,0)\in A\}. 
 
 
 
 Construct a function

 

 
 
 O ~ : 2 ℰ ⊔ ℱ ⟶ ℝ \widetilde{O}:2^{\mathcal{E}\sqcup\mathcal{F}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R} 
 
 
 
 by

 

 
 
 O ~ ​ ( A ⊔ B ) = 2 − # ​ I ​ O ​ ( π ⁡ ( A ) ) , A ⊆ ℰ , B ⊆ ℱ . \widetilde{O}(A\sqcup B)=2^{-\#I}\,O(\pi(A)),\hskip 20.00003ptA\subseteq\mathcal{E},\ B\subseteq\mathcal{F}. 
 
 
 
 Thus O ~ \widetilde{O} depends only on the part lying in the first copy
 ℰ \mathcal{E} .



 
 We first prove that O ~ \widetilde{O} satisfies the orchestra inequalities on
 ℰ ⊔ ℱ \mathcal{E}\sqcup\mathcal{F} . Let

 

 
 
 ℰ ⊔ ℱ = C 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ C t \mathcal{E}\sqcup\mathcal{F}=C_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup C_{t} 
 
 
 
 be an arbitrary set partition. For each j ∈ [ t ] j\in[t] , define

 

 
 
 D j = π ⁡ ( C j ∩ ℰ ) ⊆ I . D_{j}=\pi(C_{j}\cap\mathcal{E})\subseteq I. 
 
 
 
 The nonempty sets among D 1 , … , D t D_{1},\ldots,D_{t} are pairwise disjoint and have union
 I I . Let

 

 
 
 M = { j ∈ [ t ] : D j ≠ ∅ } , m = # ​ M . M=\{j\in[t]:D_{j}\neq\emptyset\},\hskip 20.00003ptm=\#M. 
 
 
 
 Then

 

 
 
 I = ⨆ j ∈ M D j . I=\bigsqcup_{j\in M}D_{j}. 
 
 
 
 For every H ⊆ [ t ] H\subseteq[t] , we have

 

 
 
 π ⁡ ( ( ⨆ h ∈ H C h ) ∩ ℰ ) = ⋃ h ∈ H D h = ⋃ h ∈ H ∩ M D h . \pi\left(\left(\bigsqcup_{h\in H}C_{h}\right)\cap\mathcal{E}\right)=\bigcup_{h\in H}D_{h}=\bigcup_{h\in H\cap M}D_{h}. 
 
 
 
 Hence

 

 
 
 ∑ H ⊆ [ t ] O ~ ​ ( ⨆ h ∈ H C h ) \displaystyle\sum_{H\subseteq[t]}\widetilde{O}\left(\bigsqcup_{h\in H}C_{h}\right) 
 = 2 − # ​ I ​ ∑ H ⊆ [ t ] O ⁡ ( ⋃ h ∈ H D h ) \displaystyle=2^{-\#I}\sum_{H\subseteq[t]}O\left(\bigcup_{h\in H}D_{h}\right) 
 
 
 
 
 
 = 2 − # ​ I ​  2 t − m ​ ∑ H ′ ⊆ M O ⁡ ( ⋃ h ∈ H ′ D h ) . \displaystyle=2^{-\#I}\,2^{t-m}\sum_{H^{\prime}\subseteq M}O\left(\bigcup_{h\in H^{\prime}}D_{h}\right). 
 
 (5.2) 
 
 
 Indeed, after fixing H ′ = H ∩ M H^{\prime}=H\cap M , the remaining indices in
 [ t ] ∖ M [t]\setminus M can be chosen arbitrarily, and they do not change the union
 ⋃ h ∈ H D h \bigcup_{h\in H}D_{h} , since D j = ∅ D_{j}=\emptyset for j ∉ M j\notin M .



 
 Since the nonempty D j D_{j} ’s form a set partition of I I , the sum in ( 5.2 ) is
nonnegative by the orchestra inequalities satisfied by O O . Therefore O ~ \widetilde{O} 
satisfies the orchestra inequalities on ℰ ⊔ ℱ \mathcal{E}\sqcup\mathcal{F} .



 
 We now apply Theorem 5.1 to O ~ \widetilde{O} where we set β = δ = 𝟎 \beta=\delta=\mathbf{0} .
For A ⊆ ℰ A\subseteq\mathcal{E} and B ⊆ ℱ B\subseteq\mathcal{F} , we see that

 

 
 
 s λ ⁡ ( A ) / μ ⁡ ( B ) ​ ( 𝐱 ) = s λ ⁡ ( π ⁡ ( A ) ) ​ ( 𝐱 ) , s_{\lambda(A)/\mu(B)}(\mathbf{x})=s_{\lambda(\pi(A))}(\mathbf{x}), 
 
 
 
 and

 

 
 
 s λ ⁡ ( ℰ ∖ A ) / μ ⁡ ( ℱ ∖ B ) ​ ( 𝐱 ) = s λ ⁡ ( I ∖ π ⁡ ( A ) ) ​ ( 𝐱 ) . s_{\lambda(\mathcal{E}\setminus A)/\mu(\mathcal{F}\setminus B)}(\mathbf{x})=s_{\lambda(I\setminus\pi(A))}(\mathbf{x}). 
 
 
 
 Theorem 5.1 therefore gives

 

 
 
 0 \displaystyle 0 
 ≤ s ∑ A ⊆ ℰ ∑ B ⊆ ℱ O ~ ( A ⊔ B ) s λ ⁡ ( π ⁡ ( A ) ) ( 𝐱 ) s λ ⁡ ( I ∖ π ⁡ ( A ) ) ( 𝐱 ) \displaystyle\leq_{s}\sum_{A\subseteq\mathcal{E}}\sum_{B\subseteq\mathcal{F}}\widetilde{O}(A\sqcup B)\,s_{\lambda(\pi(A))}(\mathbf{x})\,s_{\lambda(I\setminus\pi(A))}(\mathbf{x}) 
 
 
 
 
 
 = ∑ A ⊆ ℰ ∑ B ⊆ ℱ 2 − # ​ I ​ O ​ ( π ⁡ ( A ) ) ​ s λ ⁡ ( π ⁡ ( A ) ) ​ ( 𝐱 ) ​ s λ ⁡ ( I ∖ π ⁡ ( A ) ) ​ ( 𝐱 ) . \displaystyle=\sum_{A\subseteq\mathcal{E}}\sum_{B\subseteq\mathcal{F}}2^{-\#I}O(\pi(A))\,s_{\lambda(\pi(A))}(\mathbf{x})\,s_{\lambda(I\setminus\pi(A))}(\mathbf{x}). 
 
 (5.3) 
 
 
 For a fixed A ⊆ ℰ A\subseteq\mathcal{E} , the summand is independent of
 B ⊆ ℱ B\subseteq\mathcal{F} , and there are 2 # ​ I 2^{\#I} choices of B B . Hence the
normalizing factor 2 − # ​ I 2^{-\#I} cancels the sum over B B . Since
 A ↦ π ⁡ ( A ) A\mapsto\pi(A) is a bijection from subsets of ℰ \mathcal{E} to subsets of
 I I , ( 5.3 ) becomes

 

 
 
 ∑ E ⊆ I O ⁡ ( E ) ​ s λ ⁡ ( E ) ​ ( 𝐱 ) ​ s λ ⁡ ( I ∖ E ) ​ ( 𝐱 ) , \sum_{E\subseteq I}O(E)\,s_{\lambda(E)}(\mathbf{x})\,s_{\lambda(I\setminus E)}(\mathbf{x}), 
 
 
 
 as desired.
 ■ \blacksquare 





 
 6 Proof of Theorem 1.5 


 
 We will finish the proof of Theorem 1.5 in this section. The main ingredients include Corollaries 3.5 and 3.10 , Theorem 4.1 , as well as Theorem 5.2 .



 
 Theorem 6.1 (=Theorem 1.5 ) .

 
 For partitions λ , μ \lambda,\mu , we have 

 

 
 
 H λ ( 𝐱 ; 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) H μ ( 𝐱 ; 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) ≤ s 𝐭 , 𝐰 H λ ∨ μ ( 𝐱 ; 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) H λ ∧ μ ( 𝐱 ; 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) . H_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w})\,H_{\mu}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w})\leq_{s}^{\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}}H_{\lambda\vee\mu}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w})\,H_{\lambda\wedge\mu}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}). 
 
 
 




 
 Proof. 
Let l = max ⁡ { ℓ ⁡ ( λ ) , ℓ ⁡ ( μ ) } l=\max\{\ell({\lambda}),\ell(\mu)\} .
For an l l -oscillating sequence

 

 
 
 S = ( λ 0 , λ 1 , … , λ 2 ​ l − 1 ) , S=(\lambda^{0},\lambda^{1},\ldots,\lambda^{2l-1}), 
 
 
 
 define 𝐫 ⁡ ( S ) = ( r 1 ​ ( S ) , … , r 2 ​ l − 1 ​ ( S ) ) \mathbf{r}(S)=(r_{1}(S),\ldots,r_{2l-1}(S)) by setting

 

 
 
 r 2 ​ i − 1 ​ ( S ) = | λ 2 ​ i − 1 / λ 2 ​ i − 2 | ​ for  1 ≤ i ≤ l , r 2 ​ j ​ ( S ) = | λ 2 ​ j − 1 / λ 2 ​ j | ​  for  1 ≤ j ≤ l − 1 . r_{2i-1}(S)=|\lambda^{2i-1}/\lambda^{2i-2}|\ \text{for $1\leq i\leq l$},\ \ r_{2j}(S)=|\lambda^{2j-1}/\lambda^{2j}|\ \text{ for $1\leq j\leq l-1$}. 
 
 
 
 For two l l -oscillating sequences S S and S ′ S^{\prime} , it is readily checked that for 1 ≤ i ≤ 2 ​ l − 1 1\leq i\leq 2l-1 ,

 

 
 
 r i ​ ( S ) + r i ​ ( S ′ ) = r i ​ ( S ∨ S ′ ) + r i ​ ( S ∧ S ′ ) . r_{i}(S)+r_{i}(S^{\prime})=r_{i}(S\vee S^{\prime})+r_{i}(S\wedge S^{\prime}). 
 
 (6.1) 
 
 


 
 Write 𝐪 = ( q 1 , … , q 2 ​ l − 1 ) \mathbf{q}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{2l-1}) . Then

 

 
 
 𝐪 𝐫 ⁡ ( S ) = ∏ 1 ≤ i ≤ l q 2 ​ i − 1 r 2 ​ i − 1 ​ ( S ) ​ ∏ 1 ≤ j ≤ l − 1 q 2 ​ j r 2 ​ j ​ ( S ) . \mathbf{q}^{\mathbf{r}(S)}=\prod_{1\leq i\leq l}q_{2i-1}^{r_{2i-1}(S)}\prod_{1\leq j\leq l-1}q_{2j}^{r_{2j}(S)}. 
 
 
 
 For partitions λ , α \lambda,\alpha , define

 

 
 
 K λ , α ​ ( 𝐪 ) = ∑ S ∈ OS l ⁡ ( λ → α ) 𝐪 𝐫 ⁡ ( S ) . K_{\lambda,\alpha}(\mathbf{q})=\sum_{S\in\OS^{l}(\lambda\to\alpha)}\mathbf{q}^{\mathbf{r}(S)}. 
 
 
 
 For 𝐚 ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 2 ​ l − 1 \mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2l-1} , set

 

 
 
 F α 𝐚 ​ ( β ) = # ⁡ { S ∈ OS l ⁡ ( α → β ) : 𝐫 ⁡ ( S ) = 𝐚 } . F_{\alpha}^{\mathbf{a}}(\beta)=\#\{S\in\OS^{l}(\alpha\to\beta):\mathbf{r}(S)=\mathbf{a}\}. 
 
 
 
 Thus

 

 
 
 K α , β ​ ( 𝐪 ) = ∑ 𝐚 F α 𝐚 ​ ( β ) ​ 𝐪 𝐚 . K_{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbf{q})=\sum_{\mathbf{a}}F_{\alpha}^{\mathbf{a}}(\beta)\mathbf{q}^{\mathbf{a}}. 
 
 
 


 
 Let

 

 
 
 H λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐪 ) = ∑ α K λ , α ​ ( 𝐪 ) ​ s α ​ ( 𝐱 ) , {H}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{q})=\sum_{\alpha}K_{\lambda,\alpha}(\mathbf{q})s_{\alpha}(\mathbf{x}), 
 
 
 
 which, together with Corollary 3.5 , specializes to H λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) {H}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}) after the substitutions

 

 
 
 q 2 ​ i − 1 = w l − i + 1 ​ ( 1 ≤ i ≤ l ) , q 2 ​ j = t l − j ​ ( 1 ≤ j ≤ l − 1 ) . q_{2i-1}=w_{l-i+1}\ (1\leq i\leq l),\hskip 20.00003ptq_{2j}=t_{l-j}\ (1\leq j\leq l-1). 
 
 (6.2) 
 
 


 
 Remark. Strictly speaking, in Corollary 3.5 , we require l = ℓ ⁡ ( λ ) l=\ell(\lambda) . In the case when l > ℓ ⁡ ( λ ) l>\ell(\lambda) , we notice that the first 2 ​ ( l − ℓ ​ ( λ ) ) 2(l-\ell(\lambda)) shapes in any oscillating sequence in OS l ⁡ ( λ → α ) \OS^{l}(\lambda\to\alpha) are equal, and so there is a bijection from OS l ⁡ ( λ → α ) \OS^{l}(\lambda\to\alpha) to OS ℓ ⁡ ( λ ) ⁡ ( λ → α ) \OS^{\ell(\lambda)}(\lambda\to\alpha) by ignoring the initial 2 ​ ( l − ℓ ​ ( λ ) ) 2(l-\ell(\lambda)) shapes. In particular, after the substitutions in ( 6.2 ), H λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐪 ) {H}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{q}) still specializes to H λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐭 , 𝐰 ) {H}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}) .



 
 For ζ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 2 ​ l − 1 \zeta\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2l-1} , denote

 

 
 
 L ζ ​ ( ν , γ ) = ∑ 𝐚 + 𝐛 = ζ F λ 𝐚 ​ ( ν ) ​ F μ 𝐛 ​ ( γ ) , L_{\zeta}(\nu,\gamma)=\sum_{\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}=\zeta}F_{\lambda}^{\mathbf{a}}(\nu)F_{\mu}^{\mathbf{b}}(\gamma), 
 
 
 
 and

 

 
 
 R ζ ​ ( ν , γ ) = ∑ 𝐚 + 𝐛 = ζ F λ ∨ μ 𝐚 ​ ( ν ) ​ F λ ∧ μ 𝐛 ​ ( γ ) . R_{\zeta}(\nu,\gamma)=\sum_{\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}=\zeta}F_{\lambda\vee\mu}^{\mathbf{a}}(\nu)F_{\lambda\wedge\mu}^{\mathbf{b}}(\gamma). 
 
 
 
 Then the coefficient of 𝐪 ζ \mathbf{q}^{\zeta} in

 

 
 
 H λ ∨ μ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐪 ) ​ H λ ∧ μ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐪 ) − H λ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐪 ) ​ H μ ​ ( 𝐱 , 𝐪 ) H_{\lambda\vee\mu}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{q})H_{\lambda\wedge\mu}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{q})-H_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{q})H_{\mu}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{q}) 
 
 
 
 is

 

 
 
 Δ ζ = ∑ ν , γ ( R ζ ​ ( ν , γ ) − L ζ ​ ( ν , γ ) ) ​ s ν ​ s γ . \Delta_{\zeta}=\sum_{\nu,\gamma}\bigl(R_{\zeta}(\nu,\gamma)-L_{\zeta}(\nu,\gamma)\bigr)s_{\nu}s_{\gamma}. 
 
 (6.3) 
 
 
 Therefore, it is enough to prove that for every fixed ζ \zeta , Δ ζ \Delta_{\zeta} is Schur positive. The proof will be divided into three steps.



 
 Step 1.
We first explain that the sum in ( 6.3 ) is finite for each fixed
 ζ ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 2 ​ l − 1 \zeta\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2l-1} . It follows from definition that if
 F λ 𝐚 ​ ( ν ) ≠ 0 F^{\mathbf{a}}_{\lambda}(\nu)\neq 0 , then there exists

 

 
 
 S = ( λ 0 , λ 1 , … , λ 2 ​ l − 1 ) ∈ OS l ⁡ ( λ → ν ) S=(\lambda^{0},\lambda^{1},\ldots,\lambda^{2l-1})\in\OS^{l}(\lambda\to\nu) 
 
 
 
 with 𝐫 ⁡ ( S ) = 𝐚 \mathbf{r}(S)=\mathbf{a} . Recall that

 

 
 
 a 2 ​ i − 1 = | λ 2 ​ i − 1 / λ 2 ​ i − 2 | ​ ( 1 ≤ i ≤ l ) , a 2 ​ j = | λ 2 ​ j − 1 / λ 2 ​ j | ​ ( 1 ≤ j ≤ l − 1 ) . a_{2i-1}=|\lambda^{2i-1}/\lambda^{2i-2}|\ (1\leq i\leq l),\hskip 20.00003pta_{2j}=|\lambda^{2j-1}/\lambda^{2j}|\ (1\leq j\leq l-1). 
 
 
 
 Hence

 

 
 
 | ν | = | λ | + ∑ i = 1 l a 2 ​ i − 1 − ∑ j = 1 l − 1 a 2 ​ j . |\nu|=|\lambda|+\sum_{i=1}^{l}a_{2i-1}-\sum_{j=1}^{l-1}a_{2j}. 
 
 
 
 Thus, for fixed λ \lambda and 𝐚 \mathbf{a} , the endpoint shape ν \nu must have
a fixed size, and so there are finitely many such partitions ν \nu .



 
 For fixed ζ \zeta , there are only finitely many decompositions
 𝐚 + 𝐛 = ζ \mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}=\zeta . It follows that
 L ζ ​ ( ν , γ ) L_{\zeta}(\nu,\gamma) and R ζ ​ ( ν , γ ) R_{\zeta}(\nu,\gamma) are nonzero for only
finitely many pairs ( ν , γ ) (\nu,\gamma) .



 
 The following two steps will borrow some ideas in the proofs of [ 4 , Theorem 1.7] and [ 4 , Theorem 8.1] .



 
 Step 2. We now group the summands of Δ ζ \Delta_{\zeta} in ( 6.3 ) according to their join and
meet. Fix a pair of partitions σ ⊆ ρ \sigma\subseteq\rho , and consider the
fiber

 

 
 
 ℱ ( ρ , σ ) = { ( ν , γ ) : ν ∨ γ = ρ , ν ∧ γ = σ } . \mathcal{F}(\rho,\sigma)=\{(\nu,\gamma):\nu\vee\gamma=\rho,\ \nu\wedge\gamma=\sigma\}. 
 
 
 
 Note that for two pairs of partitions σ 1 ⊆ ρ 1 \sigma^{1}\subseteq\rho^{1} and σ 2 ⊆ ρ 2 \sigma^{2}\subseteq\rho^{2} , if ( σ 1 , ρ 1 ) ≠ ( σ 2 , ρ 2 ) (\sigma^{1},\rho^{1})\neq(\sigma^{2},\rho^{2}) , then

 

 
 
 ℱ ⁡ ( ρ 1 , σ 1 ) ∩ ℱ ⁡ ( ρ 2 , σ 2 ) = ∅ . \mathcal{F}(\rho^{1},\sigma^{1})\cap\mathcal{F}(\rho^{2},\sigma^{2})=\emptyset. 
 
 
 
 Hence ( 6.3 ) can be split into disjoint summands according to ℱ ⁡ ( ρ , σ ) \mathcal{F}(\rho,\sigma) .



 
 Let l ′ = max ⁡ { ℓ ⁡ ( ρ ) , ℓ ⁡ ( σ ) } l^{\prime}=\max\{\ell(\rho),\ell(\sigma)\} , and set

 

 
 
 I = { 1 , 2 , … , l ′ } . I=\{1,2,\ldots,l^{\prime}\}. 
 
 (6.4) 
 
 
 For E ⊆ I E\subseteq I , define two vectors ν E \nu_{E} and γ E = ν I ∖ E \gamma_{E}=\nu_{I\setminus E} in ℤ ≥ 0 l ′ \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{l^{\prime}} by

 

 
 
 ν E ​ ( i ) = { ρ i , i ∈ E , σ i , i ∉ E , γ E ​ ( i ) = { σ i , i ∈ E , ρ i , i ∉ E . \nu_{E}(i)=\begin{cases}\rho_{i},&i\in E,\\
\sigma_{i},&i\notin E,\end{cases}\hskip 20.00003pt\gamma_{E}(i)=\begin{cases}\sigma_{i},&i\in E,\\
\rho_{i},&i\notin E.\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 Observe that every pair ( ν , γ ) (\nu,\gamma) satisfying ν ∨ γ = ρ \nu\vee\gamma=\rho and ν ∧ γ = σ \nu\wedge\gamma=\sigma 
is obtained in exactly 2 # ⁡ { i : ρ i = σ i } 2^{\#\{i:\rho_{i}=\sigma_{i}\}} ways by taking E E satisfying that

 

 
 
 { i ∈ I : ν i = ρ i , ρ i ≠ σ i } ⊆ E ⊆ { i ∈ I : ν i = ρ i } . \{i\in I:\nu_{i}=\rho_{i},\rho_{i}\neq\sigma_{i}\}\subseteq E\subseteq\{i\in I:\nu_{i}=\rho_{i}\}. 
 
 
 
 Hence the contribution of ℱ ⁡ ( ρ , σ ) \mathcal{F}(\rho,\sigma) is

 

 
 
 Δ ζ ; ρ , σ \displaystyle\Delta_{\zeta;\rho,\sigma} 
 = ∑ ( ν , γ ) ∈ ℱ ⁡ ( ρ , σ ) ( R ζ ​ ( ν , γ ) − L ζ ​ ( ν , γ ) ) ​ s ν ​ s γ = ∑ E ⊆ I O ζ ​ ( E ) ​ s ν E ​ s γ E \displaystyle=\sum_{(\nu,\gamma)\in\mathcal{F}(\rho,\sigma)}\bigl(R_{\zeta}(\nu,\gamma)-L_{\zeta}(\nu,\gamma)\bigr)s_{\nu}s_{\gamma}=\sum_{E\subseteq I}O_{\zeta}(E)\,s_{\nu_{E}}s_{\gamma_{E}} 
 
 
 
 
 
 = ∑ E ⊆ I O ζ ​ ( E ) ​ s ν E ​ s ν I ∖ E , \displaystyle=\sum_{E\subseteq I}O_{\zeta}(E)\,s_{\nu_{E}}s_{\nu_{I\setminus E}}, 
 
 (6.5) 
 
 
 where

 

 
 
 O ζ ( E ) = 2 − # ⁡ { i : ρ i = σ i } ( R ζ ( ν E , γ E ) − L ζ ( ν E , γ E ) ) . O_{\zeta}(E)=2^{-\#\{i:\rho_{i}=\sigma_{i}\}}(R_{\zeta}(\nu_{E},\gamma_{E})-L_{\zeta}(\nu_{E},\gamma_{E})). 
 
 
 
 Here, if ν E \nu_{E} or γ E \gamma_{E} is not a partition, the
corresponding terms are understood as zero.
Now, to confirm the Schur positivity of Δ ζ \Delta_{\zeta} , it is enough to show that each Δ ζ ; ρ , σ \Delta_{\zeta;\rho,\sigma} is
Schur positive.



 
 Step 3. We apply Theorem 5.2 to prove the Schur positivity of Δ ζ ; ρ , σ \Delta_{\zeta;\rho,\sigma} in ( 6.5 ).
It suffices to verify that O ζ O_{\zeta} satisfies the orchestra
inequalities required in Theorem 5.2 .
Fix any set partition B 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ B t B_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup B_{t} of the set I I in ( 6.4 ).
For H ⊆ [ t ] H\subseteq[t] , let

 

 
 
 E H = ⋃ h ∈ H B h , ν H = ν E H , γ H = γ E H = ν E H ¯ . E_{H}=\bigcup_{h\in H}B_{h},\hskip 20.00003pt\nu_{H}=\nu_{E_{H}},\hskip 20.00003pt\gamma_{H}=\gamma_{E_{H}}=\nu_{E_{\overline{H}}}. 
 
 
 
 The orchestra
inequality we need to prove is

 

 
 
 ∑ H ⊆ [ t ] O ζ ​ ( E H ) ≥ 0 , \sum_{H\subseteq[t]}O_{\zeta}(E_{H})\geq 0, 
 
 
 
 which is equivalent to (both sides multiplied by the factor 2 # ⁡ { i : ρ i = σ i } 2^{\#\{i:\rho_{i}=\sigma_{i}\}} )

 

 
 
 ∑ H ⊆ [ t ] [ 𝐪 ζ ] ​ K λ ∨ μ , ν H ​ ( 𝐪 ) ​ K λ ∧ μ , γ H ​ ( 𝐪 ) ≥ ∑ H ⊆ [ t ] [ 𝐪 ζ ] ​ K λ , ν H ​ ( 𝐪 ) ​ K μ , γ H ​ ( 𝐪 ) . \sum_{H\subseteq[t]}[\mathbf{q}^{\zeta}]\,K_{\lambda\vee\mu,\nu_{H}}(\mathbf{q})K_{\lambda\wedge\mu,\gamma_{H}}(\mathbf{q})\geq\sum_{H\subseteq[t]}[\mathbf{q}^{\zeta}]\,K_{\lambda,\nu_{H}}(\mathbf{q})K_{\mu,\gamma_{H}}(\mathbf{q}). 
 
 
 
 We will prove a stronger version

 

 
 
 ∑ H ⊆ [ t ] K λ , ν H ​ ( 𝐪 ) ​ K μ , γ H ​ ( 𝐪 ) ≤ ∑ H ⊆ [ t ] 𝐪 ⁡ K λ ∨ μ , ν H ​ ( 𝐪 ) ​ K λ ∧ μ , γ H ​ ( 𝐪 ) . \sum_{H\subseteq[t]}K_{\lambda,\nu_{H}}(\mathbf{q})K_{\mu,\gamma_{H}}(\mathbf{q})\leq^{\mathbf{q}}\sum_{H\subseteq[t]}K_{\lambda\vee\mu,\nu_{H}}(\mathbf{q})K_{\lambda\wedge\mu,\gamma_{H}}(\mathbf{q}). 
 
 (6.6) 
 
 


 
 We use Theorem 4.1 to give a proof of ( 6.6 ).
By Step 1, we see that there exists an integer m m such that every partition
 ν \nu or γ \gamma appearing in a nonzero term of Δ ζ \Delta_{\zeta} 
satisfies
 ℓ ⁡ ( ν ) , ℓ ⁡ ( γ ) ≤ m \ell(\nu),\ell(\gamma)\leq m . Set k = max ⁡ { λ 1 , μ 1 } k=\max\{{\lambda}_{1},\mu_{1}\} .
Consider the finite distributive lattice
 OS k l , m \OS_{k}^{l,m} , as defined in ( 3.1 ).
For each H ⊆ [ t ] H\subseteq[t] , define subsets of OS k l , m \OS_{k}^{l,m} by

 

 
 
 A H \displaystyle A_{H} 
 = OS l ⁡ ( λ → ν H ) , \displaystyle=\OS^{l}(\lambda\to\nu_{H}), 
 B H \displaystyle\hskip 20.00003ptB_{H} 
 = OS l ⁡ ( μ → ν H ) , \displaystyle=\OS^{l}(\mu\to\nu_{H}), 
 
 
 
 
 C H \displaystyle C_{H} 
 = OS l ⁡ ( λ ∨ μ → ν H ) , \displaystyle=\OS^{l}(\lambda\vee\mu\to\nu_{H}), 
 D H \displaystyle\hskip 20.00003ptD_{H} 
 = OS l ⁡ ( λ ∧ μ → ν H ) . \displaystyle=\OS^{l}(\lambda\wedge\mu\to\nu_{H}). 
 
 
 
 Here when ν H \nu_{H} is not a valid partition, the above sets are understood as empty sets.
Then

 

 
 
 ∑ S ∈ A H 𝐪 𝐫 ⁡ ( S ) = K λ , ν H ​ ( 𝐪 ) , ∑ T ∈ B H 𝐪 𝐫 ⁡ ( T ) = K μ , ν H ​ ( 𝐪 ) , \sum_{S\in A_{H}}\mathbf{q}^{\mathbf{r}(S)}=K_{\lambda,\nu_{H}}(\mathbf{q}),\hskip 20.00003pt\sum_{T\in B_{H}}\mathbf{q}^{\mathbf{r}(T)}=K_{\mu,\nu_{H}}(\mathbf{q}), 
 
 
 
 and similarly for C H , D H C_{H},D_{H} .



 
 It remains to verify the hypotheses in Theorem 4.1 .
Take S ∈ A H S\in A_{H} and S ′ ∈ B J . S^{\prime}\in B_{J}. 
The initial shapes of S ∨ S ′ S\vee S^{\prime} and S ∧ S ′ S\wedge S^{\prime} are λ ∨ μ \lambda\vee\mu and λ ∧ μ \lambda\wedge\mu , and their final shapes are ν H ∨ ν J \nu_{H}\vee\nu_{J} and ν H ∧ ν J \nu_{H}\wedge\nu_{J} .
By the construction of ν H \nu_{H} , one has

 

 
 
 ν H ∨ ν J = ν H ∪ J , ν H ∧ ν J = ν H ∩ J . \nu_{H}\vee\nu_{J}=\nu_{H\cup J},\hskip 20.00003pt\nu_{H}\wedge\nu_{J}=\nu_{H\cap J}. 
 
 
 
 Therefore

 

 
 
 A H ∨ B J ⊆ C H ∪ J , A H ∧ B J ⊆ D H ∩ J . A_{H}\vee B_{J}\subseteq C_{H\cup J},\hskip 20.00003ptA_{H}\wedge B_{J}\subseteq D_{H\cap J}. 
 
 
 
 Moreover, we see from ( 6.1 ) that 𝐫 = ( r 1 , … , r 2 ​ l − 1 ) \mathbf{r}=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{2l-1}) are modular functions.
Applying Theorem 4.1 to these four families, we have

 

 
 
 ∑ H ⊆ [ t ] ( ∑ S ∈ A H 𝐪 𝐫 ⁡ ( S ) ) ​ ( ∑ T ∈ B H ¯ 𝐪 𝐫 ⁡ ( T ) ) ≤ ∑ H ⊆ [ t ] 𝐪 ⁡ ( ∑ U ∈ C H 𝐪 𝐫 ⁡ ( U ) ) ​ ( ∑ V ∈ D H ¯ 𝐪 𝐫 ⁡ ( V ) ) , \sum_{H\subseteq[t]}\left(\sum_{S\in A_{H}}\mathbf{q}^{\mathbf{r}(S)}\right)\left(\sum_{T\in B_{\overline{H}}}\mathbf{q}^{\mathbf{r}(T)}\right)\leq^{\mathbf{q}}\sum_{H\subseteq[t]}\left(\sum_{U\in C_{H}}\mathbf{q}^{\mathbf{r}(U)}\right)\left(\sum_{V\in D_{\overline{H}}}\mathbf{q}^{\mathbf{r}(V)}\right), 
 
 
 
 which, along with γ H = ν H ¯ \gamma_{H}=\nu_{\overline{H}} , is exactly ( 6.6 ).



 
 Collecting the above analysis in Steps 1, 2 and 3, we conclude that for every fixed ζ \zeta , Δ ζ \Delta_{\zeta} in ( 6.3 ) is Schur positive.
This enables us to complete the proof.
 ■ \blacksquare
